 succeeded in 294ms:
 241: A nonempty intersection of open stars is the open star of the simplex spanned
 242: by the corresponding vertices, hence is contractible.  Barycentric
 243: subdivision refines the preceding cover, and every finite basis cover is
 244: refined by a sufficiently late subdivision cover; the sequence is therefore
 245: filtered and cofinal.  The basic intersections are acyclic for the constant
 246: sheaf, so (ii), the sheaf-category equivalence, and cofinality give
 247: \[
 248:  \varinjlim_m\check H^2(\mathcal U^{(m)},A)
 249:  \cong H^2(\mathcal B_K,A)
 250:  \cong H^2(X,A).
 251: \]
 252: This proves every asserted comparison, including the passage from the
 253: all-subdivision generated site to the usual topological site.
 254: 
 255: The order of the argument is essential.  Contractibility supplies
 256: connectedness and acyclicity on each selected cover.  The basis comparison
 257: identifies sheaves on the generated site with sheaves on \(X\).  Cofinality
 258: then identifies the colimit over subdivision covers with the full Cech
 259: colimit.  Only after these three checks is the final derived-cohomology
 260: identification justified.
 261: \end{proof}
 262: 
 263: We turn to the paper-specific packaging of the standard Cech-cocycle gerbe
 264: construction.  The point is not that a class in \(H^2\) can be represented
 265: by a gerbe; that is classical.  The content is simultaneous control of the
 266: component presheaf, terminal essential surjectivity, neutral and non-neutral
 267: labels, and the images of the evaluation homomorphisms.
 268: 
 269: \begin{theorem}[Finite-site realization of prescribed componentwise evaluation maps]
 270: \label{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction}
 271: Let \(\mathcal U=\{U_i\}_{i\in I}\) be a finite good cover satisfying
 272: \eqref{eq:connected-overlap-hypothesis}, with connected nerve \(N\), and let
 273: \(A\) be a finite abelian group.  Assume
 274: \[
 275:  H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0.
 276: \]
 277: Let \(\Lambda\) be a finite nonempty set and prescribe homomorphisms
 278: \[
 279:  \phi_\lambda:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\longrightarrow A
 280:  \qquad(\lambda\in\Lambda).
 281: \]
 282: Put \(\Lambda_0=\{\lambda:\phi_\lambda=0\}\).  Then there are a presheaf
 283: \(F\) on \(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}\) and an
 284: \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-banded prestack lift
 285: \(\mathfrak P\), with stackification \(\mathfrak L\), such that:
 286: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
 287: \item \(F(a)=\Lambda_0\), \((aF)(a)\cong\Lambda\), and
 288:       \(\pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)\cong\Lambda\);
 289: \item the component gerbe \(\mathfrak G_\lambda\) has class
 290:       \(\omega_\lambda\in H^2(N,A)\) satisfying
 291:       \[
 292:       \operatorname{ev}_{\omega_\lambda}=\phi_\lambda,
 293:       \qquad
 294:       \operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{ev}_{\omega_\lambda})
 295:       =\operatorname{Im}(\phi_\lambda),
 296:       \]
 297:       and \(\mathfrak G_\lambda\) is neutral exactly when
 298:       \(\lambda\in\Lambda_0\);
 299: \item \(\mathfrak P(a)\to\mathfrak L(a)\) is essentially surjective, and
 300:       its terminal objects occur exactly in the components indexed by
 301:       \(\Lambda_0\);
 302: \item \(F\) has a strict matching family on \(\mathcal U\), and
 303:       \[
 304:       \operatorname{MWA}(F,a)
 305:       \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 306:       \Lambda_0=\varnothing.
 307:       \]
 308: \end{enumerate}
 309: \end{theorem}
 310: 
 311: \begin{proof}
 312: The universal coefficient sequence and \(H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0\) give a natural
 313: isomorphism
 314: \begin{equation}\label{eq:uct-realization-isomorphism}
 315:  \eta_A:H^2(N,A)\xrightarrow{\sim}
 316:  \operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N,\mathbb Z),A).
 317: \end{equation}
 318: For every \(\lambda\), let \(\omega_\lambda\) be the inverse image of
 319: \(\phi_\lambda\).  Choose a normalized simplicial cocycle
 320: \(\alpha_\lambda\in Z^2(N,A)\) representing it, choosing
 321: \(\alpha_\lambda=0\) when \(\lambda\in\Lambda_0\).  Extend it alternately
 322: to ordered triples and set it to zero on triples with a repeated index.
 323: 
 324: The choice \(\alpha_\lambda=0\) for a zero map is legitimate because
 325: \(\eta_A\) is injective: \(\phi_\lambda=0\) implies
 326: \(\omega_\lambda=0\).  Conversely, a nonzero prescribed map has a nonzero
 327: class.  For pairwise distinct indices, alternating extension means
 328: \[
 329:  \alpha_{\lambda,\sigma(i)\sigma(j)\sigma(k)}
 330:  =\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)\alpha_{\lambda,ijk}.
 331: \]
 332: On every ordered \(3\)-simplex, the cocycle equation is
 333: \begin{equation}\label{eq:realization-cocycle-equation}
 334:  \alpha_{\lambda,jkl}-\alpha_{\lambda,ikl}
 335:  +\alpha_{\lambda,ijl}-\alpha_{\lambda,ijk}=0.
 336: \end{equation}
 337: These are the only coefficient identities used in the groupoid
 338: construction.
 339: 
 340: Define the component presheaf by
 341: \[
 342:  F(W)=
 343:  \begin{cases}
 344:  \Lambda,&W\ne a,\varnothing\text{ is a nonempty intersection object},\\
 345:  \Lambda_0,&W=a,\\
 346:  \{*\},&W=\varnothing.
 347:  \end{cases}
 348: \]
 349: Restrictions among proper nonempty intersections are identities,
 350: restrictions from \(a\) are the inclusion
 351: \(\Lambda_0\hookrightarrow\Lambda\), and restrictions to
 352: \(\varnothing\) are unique.  A matching family on the vertex cover has a
 353: single label on every connected component of the nerve.  Since \(N\) is
 354: connected, its label is a unique element of \(\Lambda\).  Consequently
 355: \begin{equation}\label{eq:label-sheafification}
 356:  (aF)(a)\cong\Lambda.
 357: \end{equation}
 358: 
 359: Here is the sheafification calculation in detail.  A family
 360: \((\lambda_i)_{i\in I}\) with \(\lambda_i\in F(U_i)=\Lambda\) is matching
 361: exactly when
 362: \[
 363:  \lambda_i=\lambda_j
 364:  \quad\text{for every edge }\{i,j\}\in N.
 365: \]
 366: No condition is imposed by an empty overlap because both restrictions land
 367: in the singleton \(F(\varnothing)\).  Edgewise equality propagates along
 368: paths in the connected \(1\)-skeleton of \(N\), so every matching family is
 369: constant.  Conversely, each constant family is matching.  It determines a
 370: section of \(aF\) over \(a\), and distinct constants remain distinct after
 371: restriction because every nonempty cover member retains its label.
 372: 
 373: Every cover in the generated pretopology is obtained from the vertex cover
 374: by pullback and transitivity.  On a nonempty proper intersection, a generated
 375: cover contains an identity, so it neither creates nor identifies labels.
 376: It follows that the vertex-cover calculation computes all terminal sections
 377: of the sheafification.  On proper nonempty objects,
 378: \((aF)(W)=\Lambda\), and on the initial object it is a singleton.
 379: 
 380: We next construct the prestack and verify its structure rather than citing a
 381: separate realization result.  Fix \(\lambda\notin\Lambda_0\).  Put
 382: \(\mathfrak P_\lambda(a)=\varnothing\).  For a nonempty proper intersection
 383: \(W\), let
 384: \[
 385:  I(W)=\{i\in I:W\subseteq U_i\}.
 386: \]
 387: The groupoid \(\mathfrak P_\lambda(W)\) has objects
 388: \(x_i^{\lambda,W}\) for \(i\in I(W)\), and
 389: \[
 390:  \operatorname{Hom}(x_i^{\lambda,W},x_j^{\lambda,W})
 391:  =A_{\mathcal U}(W)\cong A.
 392: \]
 393: Writing a morphism as \((b,i,j)\), define
 394: \begin{equation}\label{eq:twisted-composition}
 395:  (c,j,k)\circ(b,i,j)
 396:  =(b+c+\alpha_{\lambda,ijk},i,k).
 397: \end{equation}
 398: Normalization supplies identities and inverses.  Explicitly,
 399: \[
 400:  1_{x_i^{\lambda,W}}=(0,i,i),
 401:  \qquad
 402:  (b,i,j)^{-1}=(-b,j,i),
 403: \]
 404: because every cocycle coordinate with a repeated index is zero.  For three
 405: composable arrows, the two parenthesizations are
 406: \[
 407: \begin{aligned}
 408:  (d,k,l)\circ\bigl((c,j,k)\circ(b,i,j)\bigr)
 409:  &=(b+c+d+\alpha_{\lambda,ijk}
 410:        +\alpha_{\lambda,ikl},i,l),\\
 411:  \bigl((d,k,l)\circ(c,j,k)\bigr)\circ(b,i,j)
 412:  &=(b+c+d+\alpha_{\lambda,jkl}
 413:        +\alpha_{\lambda,ijl},i,l).
 414: \end{aligned}
 415: \]
 416: They agree by \eqref{eq:realization-cocycle-equation}.  Associativity is
 417: therefore exactly the cocycle identity
 418: \[
 419:  \alpha_{\lambda,ijk}+\alpha_{\lambda,ikl}
 420:  =\alpha_{\lambda,jkl}+\alpha_{\lambda,ijl}.
 421: \]
 422: If \(V\subseteq W\), then \(I(W)\subseteq I(V)\); restriction keeps the
 423: object index and applies the sheaf restriction to the coefficient.  These
 424: functors compose strictly.  Over \(\varnothing\) take the terminal groupoid.
 425: 
 426: To verify the restriction assertion, write
 427: \(\rho_{VW}:A_{\mathcal U}(W)\to A_{\mathcal U}(V)\).  The functor sends
 428: \[
 429:  x_i^{\lambda,W}\longmapsto x_i^{\lambda,V},
 430:  \qquad
 431:  (b,i,j)\longmapsto(\rho_{VW}(b),i,j).
 432: \]
 433: On nonempty connected intersections, the coherent trivializations identify
 434: \(\rho_{VW}\) with the identity of \(A\).  Hence
 435: \[
 436:  \rho_{VW}(b+c+\alpha_{\lambda,ijk})
 437:  =\rho_{VW}(b)+\rho_{VW}(c)+\alpha_{\lambda,ijk},
 438: \]
 439: so restriction preserves \eqref{eq:twisted-composition}.  If
 440: \(Z\subseteq V\subseteq W\), the equality
 441: \(\rho_{ZV}\rho_{VW}=\rho_{ZW}\) gives literal equality of the restriction
 442: functors.  This is strict functoriality, not functoriality only up to a
 443: cartesian comparison arrow.
 444: 
 445: For fixed \(i,j\in I(W)\), the presheaf of arrows between the corresponding
 446: objects on the slice over \(W\) is
 447: \[
 448:  V\longmapsto A_{\mathcal U}(V).
 449: \]
 450: It is the restriction of the genuine coefficient sheaf, and hence is a
 451: sheaf.  This verifies morphism descent for every twisted summand.
 452: Translation in the coefficient coordinate identifies
 453: \[
 454:  \underline{\operatorname{Aut}}(x_i^{\lambda,W})
 455:  \cong A_{\mathcal U}|_{\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}/W}.
 456: \]
 457: Changing \(i\) transports this identification through an arrow
 458: \((b,i,j)\); abelianness makes the induced conjugation on \(A\) trivial.
 459: The identifications therefore constitute an \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-banding.
 460: 
 461: For \(\lambda\in\Lambda_0\), let \(\mathfrak P_\lambda\) be the neutral
 462: one-object \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-banded stack on every nonempty object and the
 463: terminal groupoid on \(\varnothing\).  This is a stack here, not merely a
 464: one-object prestack: on the slice over a nonempty formal intersection, every
 465: generated cover contains an identity, so the nonempty part of the topology
 466: is trivial; the empty cover forces value zero at \(\varnothing\).  Evaluation
 467: at the terminal slice object is exact, whence
 468: \(H^1(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}/W,A_{\mathcal U})=0\), and every object
 469: descent torsor is trivial.
 470: 
 471: Concretely, descent data for the distinguished local object on a cover
 472: \(\{V_r\to W\}\) are arrows represented by coefficients
 473: \(d_{rs}\in A_{\mathcal U}(V_{rs})\) satisfying
 474: \[
 475:  d_{rs}+d_{st}=d_{rt}.
 476: \]
 477: They form an \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-torsor cocycle.  The vanishing just noted
 478: provides coefficients \(e_r\in A_{\mathcal U}(V_r)\) with
 479: \(d_{rs}=e_s-e_r\).  Adjusting the local identifications by \(e_r\) makes
 480: them agree and yields the global object.  Morphism descent is the ordinary

 succeeded in 282ms:
   1: \section{Homological images and aggregate quotients}
   2: \label{sec:homological-images}
   3: 
   4: Fix a finite simplicial complex \(N\), a finite abelian group \(A\), and a
   5: class \(\omega\in H^2(N,A)\).  The universal coefficient map is denoted by
   6: \[
   7:  \operatorname{ev}_\omega:
   8:  H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\longrightarrow A.
   9: \]
  10: Define its \emph{homological image} and \emph{homological cokernel} by
  11: \begin{equation}\label{eq:homological-image-cokernel}
  12:  K_\omega:=\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{ev}_\omega),
  13:  \qquad Q_\omega:=A/K_\omega.
  14: \end{equation}
  15: These definitions depend on the supplied finite presentation of the component
  16: gerbe.  Refinement and retrivialization transport them by UCT naturality, but
  17: no canonical identification along distinct comparison zigzags is asserted.
  18: The resulting freely generated presentation groupoid and its elementary
  19: naturality calculation are recorded in the technical appendix rather than
  20: promoted to an invariance theorem.
  21: 
  22: For a finite family of component classes
  23: \((\omega_v)_{v\in V}\), put \(K_v=K_{\omega_v}\) and define
  24: \begin{equation}\label{eq:aggregate-quotients}
  25:  Q_{\mathrm{lab}}:=A\Big/\bigcap_{v\in V}K_v,
  26:  \qquad
  27:  Q_{\mathrm{com}}:=A\Big/\sum_{v\in V}K_v.
  28: \end{equation}
  29: The first quotient retains enough information to map to every componentwise
  30: quotient \(A/K_v\).  The second retains only characters which descend through
  31: every one of them.
  32: 
  33: \begin{proposition}[UCT and aggregate subgroup package]
  34: \label{prop:uct-aggregate-package}
  35: With the notation above:
  36: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  37: \item The universal coefficient sequence is
  38:       \[
  39:       0\longrightarrow
  40:       \operatorname{Ext}^1(H_1(N,\mathbb Z),A)
  41:       \longrightarrow H^2(N,A)
  42:       \xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}}
  43:       \operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N,\mathbb Z),A)
  44:       \longrightarrow0.
  45:       \]
  46:       Thus \(K_\omega=0\) exactly when \(\omega\) lies in the displayed
  47:       Ext subgroup.  In particular, nonzero classes can have zero
  48:       homological image when \(H_1\) has torsion.
  49: \item The quotient map \(q_\omega:A\to Q_\omega\) is initial among group
  50:       homomorphisms \(q:A\to B\) satisfying
  51:       \(q\circ\operatorname{ev}_\omega=0\).  A character
  52:       \(\chi:A\to\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z\) factors through \(Q_\omega\)
  53:       exactly when \(\chi(K_\omega)=0\).  Since finite Pontryagin duality
  54:       separates points,
  55:       \[
  56:       K_\omega=\mathop{\bigcap}_{\chi(K_\omega)=0}\ker\chi.
  57:       \]
  58: \item For a nonempty finite family \((K_v)_{v\in V}\), there is a natural
  59:       short exact sequence
  60:       \begin{equation}\label{eq:aggregate-exact-sequence}
  61:       0\longrightarrow
  62:       \frac{\sum_vK_v}{\bigcap_vK_v}
  63:       \longrightarrow Q_{\mathrm{lab}}
  64:       \longrightarrow Q_{\mathrm{com}}
  65:       \longrightarrow0.
  66:       \end{equation}
  67:       Moreover,
  68:       \[
  69:       \widehat{Q_{\mathrm{com}}}
  70:       \cong\bigcap_{v\in V}K_v^\perp,
  71:       \qquad
  72:       \widehat{Q_{\mathrm{lab}}}
  73:       \cong\sum_{v\in V}K_v^\perp
  74:       \]
  75:       inside \(\widehat A\).
  76: \item If \(H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0\) and
  77:       \(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta\), a subgroup
  78:       \(K\le A\) occurs as \(K_\omega\) exactly when \(d(K)\le\beta\).
  79:       Every finite family of such subgroups is simultaneously attained by
  80:       the prestack of
  81:       \Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction}.
  82: \end{enumerate}
  83: \end{proposition}
  84: 
  85: \begin{proof}
  86: Clause (i) is the cohomological universal coefficient theorem, natural in
  87: both \(N\) and \(A\) \cite[Sec.~3.1]{Hatcher2002}.  Its middle-to-right map
  88: evaluates a cohomology class on integral \(2\)-cycles, so its value at
  89: \(\omega\) is \(\operatorname{ev}_\omega\).  Exactness identifies the
  90: kernel with the Ext term.
  91: 
  92: For a simplicial map \(f:N'\to N\), naturality is the commutative square
  93: \[
  94: \begin{CD}
  95: H^2(N,A) @>{\operatorname{ev}}>>
  96:  \operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N,\mathbb Z),A)\\
  97: @V{f^*}VV @VV{(-)\circ f_*}V\\
  98: H^2(N',A) @>{\operatorname{ev}}>>
  99:  \operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N',\mathbb Z),A).
 100: \end{CD}
 101: \]
 102: Thus
 103: \[
 104:  \operatorname{ev}_{f^*\omega}
 105:  =\operatorname{ev}_\omega\circ f_*.
 106: \]
 107: For a coefficient homomorphism \(\theta:A\to A'\), the corresponding
 108: identity is
 109: \[
 110:  \operatorname{ev}_{\theta_*\omega}
 111:  =\theta\circ\operatorname{ev}_\omega.
 112: \]
 113: These formulas are the only invariance input used for a specified
 114: presentation comparison.  The short exact UCT sequence may split as a
 115: sequence of groups, but no natural splitting is chosen.
 116: 
 117: For (ii), \(q\circ\operatorname{ev}_\omega=0\) says precisely that \(q\)
 118: vanishes on \(K_\omega\).  The universal property of the quotient gives a
 119: unique factorization through \(A/K_\omega\).  Apply the same observation to
 120: characters.  The intersection formula follows because characters of a
 121: finite abelian group separate points \cite{Terras1999}.
 122: 
 123: For the last assertion, pass to the finite quotient \(A/K_\omega\).  If
 124: \(a\notin K_\omega\), then \(\bar a\ne0\) in that quotient.  Finite
 125: Pontryagin duality gives a character
 126: \[
 127:  \bar\chi:A/K_\omega\longrightarrow\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z
 128: \]
 129: with \(\bar\chi(\bar a)\ne0\).  Its pullback \(\chi=\bar\chi q_\omega\)
 130: annihilates \(K_\omega\) but not \(a\).  Hence no element outside
 131: \(K_\omega\) belongs to the displayed intersection of kernels.  The reverse
 132: containment is immediate.
 133: 
 134: The containment \(\bigcap_vK_v\subseteq\sum_vK_v\) gives the quotient map in
 135: \eqref{eq:aggregate-exact-sequence}.  By the third isomorphism theorem its
 136: kernel is
 137: \[
 138:  \frac{\sum_vK_v}{\bigcap_vK_v},
 139: \]
 140: which proves exactness.  For finite abelian groups, annihilators exchange sums and
 141: intersections:
 142: \[
 143:  \Big(\sum_vK_v\Big)^\perp=\bigcap_vK_v^\perp,
 144:  \qquad
 145:  \Big(\bigcap_vK_v\Big)^\perp=\sum_vK_v^\perp.
 146: \]
 147: Dualizing the two quotients proves the character formulas in (iii).
 148: 
 149: The first annihilator identity does not require finiteness:
 150: \(\chi\) kills \(\sum_vK_v\) exactly when it kills every \(K_v\).  For the
 151: second, one inclusion is immediate:
 152: \[
 153:  \sum_vK_v^\perp\subseteq\Big(\bigcap_vK_v\Big)^\perp.
 154: \]
 155: For finite groups, equality follows from exactness of Pontryagin duality.
 156: It may also be checked by cardinalities, using
 157: \[
 158:  |H^\perp|=\frac{|A|}{|H|}
 159: \]
 160: and, for two subgroups,
 161: \[
 162:  |K+L|\,|K\cap L|=|K|\,|L|.
 163: \]
 164: Induction gives the formula for a finite family.
 165: 
 166: Under the hypotheses of (iv), the Ext term vanishes and UCT becomes
 167: \[
 168:  H^2(N,A)\cong\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb Z^\beta,A).
 169: \]
 170: The image of a homomorphism from \(\mathbb Z^\beta\) is generated by the
 171: images of the standard basis, so it has at most \(\beta\) generators.
 172: Conversely, given generators \(k_1,\dots,k_\beta\) of \(K\), allowing zeros
 173: when fewer are needed, the map
 174: \[
 175:  (n_1,\dots,n_\beta)\longmapsto\sum_jn_jk_j
 176: \]
 177: has image \(K\).  Applying
 178: \Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction} to a family of these maps
 179: gives simultaneous realization.
 180: 
 181: The number \(d(K)\) can be read prime by prime.  If
 182: \[
 183:  K\cong\bigoplus_p\bigoplus_{j=1}^{r_p}C_{p^{e_{p,j}}},
 184: \]
 185: then
 186: \[
 187:  d(K)=\max_p r_p.
 188: \]
 189: Thus the condition in (iv) is not a bound on \(|K|\) or on its exponent.
 190: It is precisely the rank bound imposed by the \(\beta\) basis elements of
 191: \(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\).  Once homomorphisms with the desired images have been
 192: chosen componentwise, simultaneous attainment is the prestack-level part
 193: supplied by \Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction}.
 194: \end{proof}
 195: 
 196: \subsection*{How to read the aggregate quotients}
 197: 
 198: There are canonical maps
 199: \[
 200:  Q_{\mathrm{lab}}\longrightarrow A/K_v
 201:  \qquad(v\in V),
 202: \]
 203: because \(\bigcap_wK_w\subseteq K_v\).  The product of these maps is
 204: injective:
 205: \begin{equation}\label{eq:labelled-product-embedding}
 206:  A/\bigcap_vK_v
 207:  \longrightarrow\prod_{v\in V}A/K_v,
 208:  \qquad
 209:  a+\bigcap_vK_v\longmapsto(a+K_v)_v.
 210: \end{equation}
 211: Its kernel consists of the elements lying in every \(K_v\).  Thus
 212: \(Q_{\mathrm{lab}}\) is the part of the coefficient group retained by all
 213: componentwise quotient values together.
 214: 
 215: In the other direction, every componentwise quotient maps onto
 216: \(Q_{\mathrm{com}}\):
 217: \[
 218:  A/K_v\longrightarrow A/\sum_wK_w,
 219: \]
 220: because \(K_v\subseteq\sum_wK_w\).  This is the largest quotient of \(A\)

 succeeded in 305ms:
   1: \section{Finite good-cover sites and prescribed realization}
   2: \label{sec:homological-visibility}
   3: 
   4: Let \(\mathcal U=\{U_i\}_{i\in I}\) be a finite open cover of a space
   5: \(X\).  Its ordinary nerve \(N=N(\mathcal U)\) records which finite
   6: intersections are nonempty.  In order to identify Cech cochains with one
   7: copy of a coefficient group for each simplex of \(N\), we impose the
   8: following hypothesis throughout this section:
   9: \begin{equation}\label{eq:connected-overlap-hypothesis}
  10:  \begin{minipage}{0.82\textwidth}
  11:  every nonempty finite intersection \(U_J=\bigcap_{j\in J}U_j\) is
  12:  connected, and the chosen trivializations of the locally constant
  13:  coefficient sheaf identify \(\Gamma(U_J,\mathscr A)\) coherently with
  14:  the same group \(A\).
  15:  \end{minipage}
  16: \end{equation}
  17: Without connectedness,
  18: \(\Gamma(U_J,\mathscr A)\cong A^{\pi_0(U_J)}\), whereas the ordinary nerve
  19: retains only the information that \(U_J\) is nonempty.  Thus
  20: \eqref{eq:connected-overlap-hypothesis} is essential for the ordinary-nerve
  21: formulation below.  A component nerve or local coefficients would be needed
  22: without it.
  23: 
  24: For a fixed cover satisfying \eqref{eq:connected-overlap-hypothesis}, define
  25: the \emph{finite intersection site} \(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}\) as follows.
  26: Its objects are a terminal object \(a\), one object \(U_J\) for every
  27: nonempty simplex \(J\) of \(N\), and a strict initial object \(\varnothing\)
  28: representing all empty intersections.  There is an arrow
  29: \(U_J\to U_K\) when \(K\subseteq J\), and
  30: \[
  31:  U_J\times_{U_L}U_K=
  32:  \begin{cases}
  33:  U_{J\cup K},&J\cup K\in N,\\
  34:  \varnothing,&J\cup K\notin N.
  35:  \end{cases}
  36: \]
  37: The pretopology is generated by the vertex cover \(\{U_i\to a\}\), its
  38: pullbacks, identity covers, and the empty cover of \(\varnothing\).  Let
  39: \(A_{\mathcal U}\) be the restriction of the sheaf of locally constant
  40: \(A\)-valued functions on \(X\).  Hence
  41: \[
  42:  A_{\mathcal U}(U_J)=A,
  43:  \qquad A_{\mathcal U}(\varnothing)=0,
  44: \]
  45: under the coherent trivializations in
  46: \eqref{eq:connected-overlap-hypothesis}.
  47: 
  48: \subsection*{Pullbacks, generated covers, and the initial object}
  49: 
  50: The formal initial object is part of the site, even though it contributes
  51: zero to the nonempty Cech nerve.  For an arbitrary finite subset
  52: \(J\subseteq I\), write
  53: \[
  54:  \widetilde U_J=
  55:  \begin{cases}
  56:  U_J,&J\in N,\\
  57:  \varnothing,&J\notin N.
  58:  \end{cases}
  59: \]
  60: If \(U_J\to U_L\) and \(U_K\to U_L\), then
  61: \[
  62:  U_J\times_{U_L}U_K=\widetilde U_{J\cup K}.
  63: \]
  64: Indeed, if \(J\cup K\) is a simplex, every intersection object mapping to
  65: both factors is indexed by a simplex containing \(J\cup K\), and therefore
  66: factors uniquely through \(U_{J\cup K}\).  If it is not a simplex, no
  67: nonempty object maps to both factors and the universal object is
  68: \(\varnothing\).  Pullbacks involving \(\varnothing\) are again
  69: \(\varnothing\).  Thus disjoint cover members still have a fibre product in
  70: the site.
  71: 
  72: Pulling the vertex cover back along \(U_J\to a\) gives
  73: \begin{equation}\label{eq:formal-pulled-back-cover}
  74:  \{\widetilde U_{J\cup\{i\}}\longrightarrow U_J\}_{i\in I}.
  75: \end{equation}
  76: Terms equal to \(\varnothing\) may be repeated, but they are not silently
  77: deleted from the pullback calculation.  They make no contribution to
  78: matching for \(A_{\mathcal U}\) because the empty cover forces
  79: \(A_{\mathcal U}(\varnothing)=0\).  The generated pretopology is obtained
  80: by iterating \eqref{eq:formal-pulled-back-cover}, identity covers, and
  81: transitivity.
  82: 
  83: There is a useful simplification away from the terminal object.  If
  84: \(U_J\ne a,\varnothing\), then the pulled-back family contains the identity
  85: of \(U_J\): choose any \(i\in J\), for which
  86: \(\widetilde U_{J\cup\{i\}}=U_J\).  Every generated cover of a nonempty
  87: object in the slice over \(U_J\) consequently refines a cover containing an
  88: identity.  Descent for an abelian sheaf on this slice is therefore controlled
  89: by its value at the terminal slice object.  Evaluation at that object is an
  90: exact functor, and
  91: \begin{equation}\label{eq:formal-slice-acyclicity}
  92:  H^q(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}/U_J,A_{\mathcal U})=0
  93:  \qquad(q>0).
  94: \end{equation}
  95: This supplies the Leray acyclicity internally for the formal good-cover
  96: site.  If one begins with an actual topological good cover instead, the same
  97: vanishing follows because every nonempty intersection is contractible.
  98: 
  99: \subsection*{Why connectedness is the exact ordinary-nerve hypothesis}
 100: 
 101: For a sheaf of locally constant \(A\)-valued functions,
 102: \[
 103:  \Gamma(U_J,A_X)\cong
 104:  \prod_{C\in\pi_0(U_J)}A.
 105: \]
 106: An ordinary \(q\)-simplex \(J=\{i_0,\dots,i_q\}\) of \(N\) records only
 107: that \(U_J\) is nonempty.  It does not record the set
 108: \(\pi_0(U_J)\).  Hence an ordinary simplicial cochain assigns one element of
 109: \(A\) to \(J\), while a Cech cochain assigns one element for every connected
 110: component of \(U_J\).  The complexes coincide precisely under coherent
 111: identifications
 112: \[
 113:  \Gamma(U_J,A_X)\cong A
 114: \]
 115: on every nonempty overlap.  Connectedness supplies the single copy, and the
 116: chosen trivializations ensure that all face restrictions are the identity
 117: of the same coefficient group.  This explains both parts of
 118: \eqref{eq:connected-overlap-hypothesis}; neither is only a notational
 119: convenience.
 120: 
 121: Under these identifications, an ordered Cech cochain
 122: \(c\in\check C^q(\mathcal U,A_{\mathcal U})\) is the family
 123: \[
 124:  c_{i_0\cdots i_q}\in A
 125:  \qquad
 126:  (i_0<\cdots<i_q,\ U_{i_0\cdots i_q}\ne\varnothing).
 127: \]
 128: Its differential is
 129: \begin{equation}\label{eq:cech-simplicial-differential}
 130:  (\delta c)_{i_0\cdots i_{q+1}}
 131:  =\sum_{r=0}^{q+1}(-1)^r
 132:  c_{i_0\cdots\widehat{i_r}\cdots i_{q+1}},
 133: \end{equation}
 134: which is exactly the simplicial coboundary of \(N\).  If an overlap is
 135: empty, its section group is zero and there is no corresponding simplex.
 136: This gives the cochain-level, rather than merely cohomological,
 137: identification used by the realization theorem.
 138: 
 139: \begin{proposition}[Connected-overlap and basis-site comparison]
 140: \label{prop:finite-site-comparison}
 141: Let \(A\) be an abelian group.
 142: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
 143: \item For a finite cover satisfying
 144:       \eqref{eq:connected-overlap-hypothesis}, the ordered Cech complex of
 145:       \(A_{\mathcal U}\) is naturally the simplicial cochain complex of the
 146:       ordinary nerve:
 147:       \[
 148:       \check C^q(\mathcal U,A_{\mathcal U})
 149:       \cong C^q(N,A).
 150:       \]
 151: \item If the nonempty intersections are also
 152:       \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-acyclic, the Leray comparison gives
 153:       \[
 154:       H^q(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U},A_{\mathcal U})
 155:       \cong \check H^q(\mathcal U,A_{\mathcal U})
 156:       \cong H^q(N,A).
 157:       \]
 158: \item Let \(K\) be a finite simplicial complex, put \(X=|K|\), and let
 159:       \(\mathcal U^{(m)}\) be the open-star cover of
 160:       \(\operatorname{Sd}^mK\).  The category \(\mathcal B_K\) of all open
 161:       stars in these covers and all their finite intersections is a basis
 162:       for \(X\), stable under finite intersections after adjoining the empty
 163:       set.  The topology generated on \(\mathcal B_K\) is the induced
 164:       open-cover topology, and restriction gives an equivalence
 165:       \[
 166:       \mathbf {Sh}(X)\simeq\mathbf {Sh}(\mathcal B_K).
 167:       \]
 168:       Under this equivalence the genuine constant sheaf on
 169:       \(\mathcal B_K\) is the restriction of the ordinary constant sheaf on
 170:       \(X\).  Every nonempty basic intersection is contractible, the covers
 171:       \((\mathcal U^{(m)})_{m\ge0}\) are filtered and cofinal, and
 172:       \[
 173:       \varinjlim_m\check H^2(\mathcal U^{(m)},A)
 174:       \cong H^2(X,A).
 175:       \]
 176: \end{enumerate}
 177: \end{proposition}
 178: 
 179: \begin{proof}
 180: For (i), an ordered Cech \(q\)-cochain assigns a section of
 181: \(A_{\mathcal U}\) to each nonempty ordered \((q+1)\)-fold intersection.
 182: Connectedness and the coherent trivializations identify that section group
 183: with one copy of \(A\), and the restriction maps with the identity of
 184: \(A\).  Empty intersections have section group zero.  The ordered Cech
 185: coboundary is therefore exactly the alternating simplicial coboundary
 186: \eqref{eq:cech-simplicial-differential}.  The identification commutes with
 187: restriction to a subcover and with every refinement map compatible with the
 188: chosen coefficient trivializations.
 189: 
 190: For (ii), the Cech-to-derived spectral sequence for \(\mathcal U\) has
 191: \[
 192:  E_1^{p,q}=\prod_{i_0<\cdots<i_p}
 193:  H^q(U_{i_0\cdots i_p},A_{\mathcal U}).
 194: \]
 195: Acyclicity makes every row with \(q>0\) vanish, so the edge map is an
 196: isomorphism.  Clause (i) identifies its remaining row with the nerve
 197: cochain complex; this is the usual Leray theorem
 198: \cite[Chap.~III, Sec.~4]{Bredon1997}.
 199: 
 200: For the formal intersection site, the same vanishing is visible directly in
 201: \eqref{eq:formal-slice-acyclicity}.  The augmented Cech resolution
 202: \[
 203:  0\longrightarrow A_{\mathcal U}
 204:  \longrightarrow
 205:  \prod_i(j_i)_*A_{\mathcal U}|_{U_i}
 206:  \longrightarrow
 207:  \prod_{i<j}(j_{ij})_*A_{\mathcal U}|_{U_{ij}}
 208:  \longrightarrow\cdots
 209: \]
 210: has acyclic terms because every nonempty intersection slice has exact global
 211: sections.  Applying global sections computes derived cohomology by the same
 212: ordered complex.  This makes clear that the comparison is a property of the
 213: specified site and band, not of an abstract nerve alone.
 214: 
 215: For (iii), open stars in successive barycentric subdivisions have arbitrarily
 216: small mesh.  Given \(x\in O\) with \(O\subseteq X\) open, a sufficiently
 217: fine subdivision has a vertex whose open star contains \(x\) and lies in
 218: \(O\).  Hence the union of all these stars is a basis.  Adding their finite
 219: intersections makes it stable under intersections, and a family of basis
 220: objects covers precisely when their union covers in \(X\).  Thus its
 221: generated topology is the topology induced from the open-set site.
 222: 
 223: To make the small-mesh assertion explicit, fix a metric on the compact
 224: polyhedron \(X\).  Barycentric subdivision reduces the mesh of each simplex
 225: by a uniform factor strictly less than one.  Choose
 226: \(\varepsilon>0\) with \(B(x,\varepsilon)\subseteq O\).  At a sufficiently
 227: late subdivision, every simplex meeting \(x\) has diameter less than
 228: \(\varepsilon/2\).  A vertex belonging to a smallest simplex whose closure
 229: contains \(x\) then has open star contained in \(O\).  Applying this
 230: argument to every member of a finite open cover and using compactness shows
 231: that a sufficiently late star cover refines that cover.
 232: 
 233: The Comparison Lemma for a basis stable under intersections now gives the
 234: displayed equivalence of sheaf categories; explicitly, restriction is fully
 235: faithful because sections are recovered by gluing over basis covers, and its
 236: quasi-inverse sends a basis sheaf to the sheaf whose sections on an open set
 237: are compatible basis-local sections.  This also shows directly that locally
 238: constant \(A\)-valued functions restrict to the genuine constant sheaf used
 239: on \(\mathcal B_K\) \cite[Chap.~III, Sec.~7]{MacLaneMoerdijk1994}.
 240: 

 succeeded in 294ms:
 481: sheaf condition for \(A_{\mathcal U}\).  Thus the neutral summand is already
 482: a stack on every relevant slice.
 483: 
 484: Set
 485: \[
 486:  \mathfrak P=\coprod_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathfrak P_\lambda
 487: \]
 488: fibrewise on nonempty objects, with a shared terminal fibre over
 489: \(\varnothing\).  For two objects in a twisted fibre, the isomorphism
 490: presheaf on the relevant slice is the restriction of the genuine sheaf
 491: \(A_{\mathcal U}\); it is therefore a sheaf.  Automorphism sheaves are the
 492: same sheaf, so \(\mathfrak P\) is an \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-banded prestack.
 493: Every nonempty proper fibre has connected components indexed by \(\Lambda\),
 494: the terminal fibre has components \(\Lambda_0\), and the empty fibre has one
 495: component.  Thus
 496: \[
 497:  \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P)\cong F.
 498: \]
 499: Stackification and \eqref{eq:label-sheafification} prove (i).
 500: 
 501: There are two points in this coproduct verification.  First, over a nonempty
 502: object there are no arrows between different \(\lambda\)-summands, so
 503: isomorphism descent cannot change a label.  A matching object descent datum
 504: therefore lies in one summand on each connected part of a cover, and the
 505: connected nerve forces a single label.  Second, all summands restrict to the
 506: same terminal groupoid over \(\varnothing\).  This causes no identification
 507: on a nonempty overlap: the initial object has no arrow back to a nonempty
 508: object and its unique descent datum contributes no coefficient.
 509: 
 510: For a twisted summand, every two named objects are isomorphic, since their
 511: Hom set is the nonempty set \(A\).  Thus each such fibre is connected.
 512: The neutral summand has one object and is connected as well.  It follows
 513: objectwise that the component presheaf is exactly \(F\), including its
 514: restriction \(\Lambda_0\hookrightarrow\Lambda\) out of the terminal object.
 515: The standard compatibility of stackification with components gives
 516: \[
 517:  \pi_0(\mathfrak L)\cong aF,
 518: \]
 519: and the terminal component sections are the labels in \(\Lambda\).
 520: 
 521: On \(U_i\), choose \(x_i^{\lambda,U_i}\); on \(U_{ij}\), choose the arrow
 522: \((0,i,j)\).  By \eqref{eq:twisted-composition}, its triple-overlap defect is
 523: \[
 524:  (0,i,k)^{-1}(0,j,k)(0,i,j)
 525:  =\alpha_{\lambda,ijk}
 526: \]
 527: under the band identification.  The Cech representative of the component
 528: gerbe is therefore exactly \(\alpha_\lambda\).  The good-cover comparison
 529: in \Cref{prop:finite-site-comparison} identifies its Giraud class with
 530: \(\omega_\lambda\), and
 531: \eqref{eq:uct-realization-isomorphism} gives
 532: \(\operatorname{ev}_{\omega_\lambda}=\phi_\lambda\).  By the standard
 533: component-gerbe package, this gerbe is neutral exactly when its class is zero,
 534: equivalently when \(\phi_\lambda=0\).  This proves (ii).
 535: 
 536: To check the sign in the defect, use
 537: \[
 538:  (0,i,k)^{-1}=(0,k,i)
 539: \]
 540: and apply \eqref{eq:twisted-composition} twice.  Normalization cancels the
 541: terms with repeated indices, while alternating extension moves the remaining
 542: term to the ordered coordinate \(\alpha_{\lambda,ijk}\).  This is the same
 543: convention as \eqref{eq:giraud-triple-defect}.  Therefore the class obtained
 544: by stackification is not merely cohomologous to a class with the desired
 545: evaluation: its chosen vertex-cover representative is the initially
 546: selected cocycle \(\alpha_\lambda\).
 547: 
 548: The connected-overlap hypothesis enters here a second time.  It identifies
 549: the automorphism section on \(U_{ijk}\) with one element of \(A\), so the
 550: defect is an ordinary simplicial coordinate.  If \(U_{ijk}\) were
 551: disconnected, the same calculation would yield a tuple in
 552: \(A^{\pi_0(U_{ijk})}\), and the prescribed ordinary-nerve cocycle would not
 553: specify the entire defect.
 554: 
 555: For \(\lambda\in\Lambda_0\), the neutral summand is already a stack and its
 556: terminal object maps to the corresponding terminal object of
 557: \(\mathfrak L\).  If \(\lambda\notin\Lambda_0\), the component gerbe is
 558: non-neutral and therefore has no object over \(a\).  Hence every object of
 559: \(\mathfrak L(a)\) lies in a zero component and comes from
 560: \(\mathfrak P(a)\), proving (iii), including the vacuous case
 561: \(\Lambda_0=\varnothing\).
 562: 
 563: When \(\Lambda_0\ne\varnothing\), an object of
 564: \(\mathfrak L(a)\) has some component label
 565: \(\lambda\in\Lambda\).  The component-gerbe package identifies the full
 566: substack at that label with \(\mathfrak G_\lambda\).  If
 567: \(\lambda\notin\Lambda_0\), non-neutrality says that this full substack has
 568: no terminal object, so the assumed object cannot have that label.  If
 569: \(\lambda\in\Lambda_0\), the distinguished terminal object of the neutral
 570: summand of \(\mathfrak P\) maps to an object in the same component of
 571: \(\mathfrak L\).  Any other terminal object in that component is isomorphic
 572: to it because the neutral component is a gerbe.  This is precisely essential
 573: surjectivity, rather than equality of object sets.
 574: 
 575: Finally, assigning one fixed \(\lambda\in\Lambda\) to every \(U_i\) is a
 576: strict matching family: restrictions on nonempty overlaps are identities and
 577: those on empty overlaps are unique.  It has an amalgamation precisely when
 578: \(F(a)=\Lambda_0\) is nonempty.  This proves (iv) and completes the
 579: construction.
 580: 
 581: In terms of the definition from \Cref{sec:preliminaries}, the last
 582: equivalence uses only existence.  If \(\Lambda_0=\varnothing\), every
 583: constant label gives a strict matching family and there is no global
 584: section, so matching without amalgamation holds.  If
 585: \(\Lambda_0\ne\varnothing\), \(F(a)\) is nonempty and the predicate
 586: \(\operatorname{MWA}(F,a)\) is false, although matching families with
 587: nonzero labels may still fail individually to amalgamate.  The theorem does
 588: not claim that every matching family amalgamates in this case.
 589: \end{proof}
 590: 
 591: The gerbes in this proof are obtained from the standard Cech-cocycle
 592: construction.  What the theorem adds is the single finite-label prestack
 593: whose sheaf of components, terminal fibre, neutrality pattern, and homological
 594: images are all prescribed simultaneously.
 595: 
 596: \subsection*{The two terminal-fibre endpoints}
 597: 
 598: If every prescribed map is zero, then \(\Lambda_0=\Lambda\).  The component
 599: presheaf is already constant on every nonempty object, all summands are
 600: neutral, and the stackification has a terminal object in every global
 601: component.  The theorem reduces to a finite coproduct of split
 602: \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-banded stacks.  Matching families and global presheaf
 603: sections are both indexed by \(\Lambda\).
 604: 
 605: At the other endpoint, suppose every prescribed map is nonzero.  Then
 606: \(\Lambda_0=\varnothing\), so
 607: \[
 608:  F(a)=\varnothing,\qquad
 609:  (aF)(a)=\Lambda,\qquad
 610:  \mathfrak P(a)=\varnothing.
 611: \]
 612: Every global component gerbe is non-neutral, hence
 613: \(\mathfrak L(a)=\varnothing\) as well.  The map
 614: \[
 615:  \mathfrak P(a)\longrightarrow\mathfrak L(a)
 616: \]
 617: is essentially surjective because both fibres are empty.  This is the
 618: vacuous terminal-essential-surjectivity case used by matching without
 619: amalgamation.  It is not a claim that the non-neutral global component
 620: sections have terminal objects; they do not.
 621: 
 622: For a mixed family, \(\mathfrak L(a)\) contains objects exactly in the
 623: components indexed by \(\Lambda_0\).  The nonzero labels still occur as
 624: sections of \(\pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)\cong(aF)(a)\), and their full component
 625: substacks are locally inhabited gerbes without terminal objects.  This is
 626: the concrete distinction among the three terminal levels in
 627: \eqref{eq:three-terminal-levels}.
 628: 
 629: \subsection*{A three-label calculation}
 630: 
 631: Take a triangulated \(2\)-sphere and an open-star cover of a barycentric
 632: subdivision.  Its nerve \(N\) satisfies
 633: \[
 634:  H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0,\qquad H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z.
 635: \]
 636: Let \(A=\mathbb Z/6\mathbb Z\), and take the label set
 637: \[
 638:  \Lambda=\{\mathtt n,\mathtt p,\mathtt q\}.
 639: \]
 640: Prescribe
 641: \[
 642: \begin{aligned}
 643:  \phi_{\mathtt n}(1)&=0,\\
 644:  \phi_{\mathtt p}(1)&=2,\\
 645:  \phi_{\mathtt q}(1)&=3.
 646: \end{aligned}
 647: \]
 648: Then
 649: \[
 650:  \Lambda_0=\{\mathtt n\},\qquad
 651:  K_{\mathtt n}=0,\qquad
 652:  K_{\mathtt p}=\langle2\rangle,\qquad
 653:  K_{\mathtt q}=\langle3\rangle.
 654: \]
 655: The three image groups have orders \(1,3,2\), respectively.  The UCT
 656: isomorphism chooses three classes in \(H^2(N,A)\cong A\), and normalized
 657: cocycles \(\alpha_{\mathtt n}=0\),
 658: \(\alpha_{\mathtt p}\), and \(\alpha_{\mathtt q}\) feed the twisted
 659: composition formula.
 660: 
 661: The component presheaf has
 662: \[
 663:  F(a)=\{\mathtt n\},
 664:  \qquad
 665:  F(U_J)=\{\mathtt n,\mathtt p,\mathtt q\}
 666: \]
 667: on every proper nonempty intersection.  Its sheafification has three
 668: terminal component sections.  Only the \(\mathtt n\)-component has a
 669: terminal stack object.  Therefore
 670: \[
 671:  \pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)
 672:  =\{\mathtt n,\mathtt p,\mathtt q\},
 673:  \qquad
 674:  \pi_0(\mathfrak L(a))=\{\mathtt n\}.
 675: \]
 676: The notation on the right means the set of isomorphism classes of actual
 677: terminal objects; it is smaller than the value at \(a\) of the component
 678: sheaf.
 679: 
 680: For the two nonzero labels,
 681: \[
 682:  Q_{\mathtt p}=A/\langle2\rangle\cong\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z,
 683:  \qquad
 684:  Q_{\mathtt q}=A/\langle3\rangle\cong\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z.
 685: \]
 686: Their images are complementary:
 687: \[
 688:  \langle2\rangle\cap\langle3\rangle=0,
 689:  \qquad
 690:  \langle2\rangle+\langle3\rangle=A.
 691: \]
 692: If all three labels are aggregated, the zero image from
 693: \(\mathtt n\) keeps the intersection equal to zero, while the sum remains
 694: \(A\).  Hence
 695: \[
 696:  Q_{\mathrm{lab}}=A,\qquad Q_{\mathrm{com}}=0.
 697: \]
 698: 
 699: This mixed example does not satisfy matching without amalgamation because
 700: \(F(a)\ne\varnothing\).  It does contain matching families with labels
 701: \(\mathtt p\) and \(\mathtt q\) which individually have no amalgamation.
 702: Deleting the neutral label produces the all-nonzero endpoint above: the same
 703: two component classes remain, \(F(a)\) becomes empty, and the existence
 704: predicate \(\operatorname{MWA}(F,a)\) becomes true.

 succeeded in 284ms:
 221: of the displayed form receiving the quotient map from every \(A/K_v\).
 222: The map in \eqref{eq:aggregate-exact-sequence} measures the loss between
 223: keeping the tuple of component values and keeping only their common quotient.
 224: 
 225: On characters, the directions reverse.  A character of
 226: \(Q_{\mathrm{com}}\) must kill every \(K_v\), while a character of
 227: \(Q_{\mathrm{lab}}\) is a finite sum of characters each killing one of the
 228: component subgroups.  Written inside \(\widehat A\), these are precisely the
 229: two annihilator identities in the proposition.  This interpretation uses
 230: only the contravariance of finite duality.
 231: 
 232: \subsection*{Evaluation at chain level}
 233: 
 234: Choose a simplicial cocycle
 235: \(\alpha\in Z^2(N,A)\) representing \(\omega\).  If
 236: \[
 237:  z=\sum_{\sigma\in N_2}n_\sigma\sigma
 238: \]
 239: is an integral \(2\)-cycle, then
 240: \begin{equation}\label{eq:chain-level-evaluation}
 241:  \operatorname{ev}_\omega([z])
 242:  =\langle\alpha,z\rangle
 243:  =\sum_{\sigma\in N_2}n_\sigma\alpha(\sigma).
 244: \end{equation}
 245: Replacing \(\alpha\) by \(\alpha+\delta b\) changes the last expression by
 246: \[
 247:  \langle\delta b,z\rangle
 248:  =\langle b,\partial z\rangle=0.
 249: \]
 250: Replacing \(z\) by \(z+\partial c\) changes it by
 251: \[
 252:  \langle\alpha,\partial c\rangle
 253:  =\langle\delta\alpha,c\rangle=0.
 254: \]
 255: Thus \eqref{eq:chain-level-evaluation} depends only on the cohomology class
 256: and the homology class.  In the realization theorem,
 257: \(\alpha=\alpha_\lambda\) is also the triple-overlap defect of the
 258: \(\lambda\)-component gerbe, so the formula evaluates that defect directly
 259: on a \(2\)-cycle of the chosen nerve.
 260: 
 261: This chain formula also explains why the coordinate subgroup
 262: \[
 263:  H_\alpha=\langle\alpha(\sigma):\sigma\in N_2\rangle
 264: \]
 265: can be larger than \(K_\omega\).  Every value in \(K_\omega\) is an integral
 266: combination of cocycle coordinates, so \(K_\omega\subseteq H_\alpha\).
 267: But the coefficients in \eqref{eq:chain-level-evaluation} must satisfy the
 268: cycle equations.  An arbitrary combination of coordinates need not arise
 269: from a cycle.  Moreover, \(H_\alpha\) can change when \(\alpha\) is altered
 270: by a coboundary, whereas \(K_\omega\) cannot.
 271: 
 272: \subsection*{Torsion and free-homology boundary cases}
 273: 
 274: Suppose
 275: \[
 276:  H_1(N,\mathbb Z)
 277:  \cong\mathbb Z^r\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^t\mathbb Z/n_j\mathbb Z.
 278: \]
 279: The free summand contributes nothing to the Ext term, while
 280: \[
 281:  \operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathbb Z/n_j\mathbb Z,A)
 282:  \cong A/n_jA.
 283: \]
 284: Consequently,
 285: \begin{equation}\label{eq:explicit-ext-term}
 286:  \operatorname{Ext}^1(H_1(N,\mathbb Z),A)
 287:  \cong\bigoplus_{j=1}^t A/n_jA.
 288: \end{equation}
 289: If every multiplication map \(n_j:A\to A\) is surjective, the Ext term
 290: vanishes even though \(H_1\) has torsion.  For finite \(A\), shared primary
 291: torsion between \(H_1\) and \(A\) is what permits classes that evaluation on
 292: \(H_2\) cannot detect.
 293: 
 294: For example, if
 295: \[
 296:  H_1(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z,
 297:  \qquad H_2(N,\mathbb Z)=0,
 298:  \qquad A=\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z,
 299: \]
 300: then \(H^2(N,A)\cong\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z\) entirely through
 301: \eqref{eq:explicit-ext-term}.  The nonzero class defines a non-neutral
 302: banded gerbe, but
 303: \[
 304:  \operatorname{ev}_\omega:0\longrightarrow A
 305: \]
 306: has zero image and \(Q_\omega=A\).  The quotient does not detect neutrality
 307: in this case.
 308: 
 309: At the opposite endpoint, if \(H_1(N,\mathbb Z)\) is free, the Ext term
 310: vanishes.  Then
 311: \[
 312:  H^2(N,A)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N,\mathbb Z),A)
 313: \]
 314: is an isomorphism.  A class has zero homological image only when it is zero.
 315: The wedge-of-spheres application lies in this regime.
 316: 
 317: \subsection*{A two-component arithmetic check}
 318: 
 319: Let
 320: \[
 321:  A=\mathbb Z/12\mathbb Z,\qquad
 322:  K_1=\langle4\rangle,\qquad
 323:  K_2=\langle3\rangle.
 324: \]
 325: Then \(K_1\) has order \(3\), \(K_2\) has order \(4\), and
 326: \[
 327:  K_1\cap K_2=0,\qquad K_1+K_2=A.
 328: \]
 329: Hence
 330: \[
 331:  Q_{\mathrm{lab}}=A,\qquad Q_{\mathrm{com}}=0,
 332: \]
 333: and the kernel in \eqref{eq:aggregate-exact-sequence} is \(A\).  Both
 334: subgroups are cyclic, so each is the image of a homomorphism
 335: \(\mathbb Z\to A\).  On a nerve with
 336: \(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z\) and \(H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0\), the
 337: prescribed-realization theorem places these maps in two component gerbes of
 338: a single prestack.
 339: 
 340: By contrast, if \(A=\mathbb Z/8\mathbb Z\), any two nonzero subgroups are
 341: comparable:
 342: \[
 343:  \langle4\rangle\subseteq\langle2\rangle\subseteq A.
 344: \]
 345: Their intersection cannot be zero.  Thus no two nonzero component images
 346: can satisfy \(K_1\cap K_2=0\) and \(K_1+K_2=A\).  This elementary contrast
 347: is the cyclic part of the arithmetic used in the next section.
 348: 
 349: All clauses except simultaneous prestack realization are standard formal
 350: consequences of UCT, finite duality, and subgroup arithmetic.  In particular,
 351: there is no separate information-theoretic theorem hidden in the fibres of
 352: these quotients.  Since a quotient fibre is a coset of its kernel, the minimum
 353: cardinality of an unrestricted auxiliary set capable of separating every
 354: fibre is simply the kernel cardinality.  For example,
 355: \[
 356:  |q_\omega^{-1}(\bar a)|=|K_\omega|,
 357:  \qquad
 358:  \left|\ker(Q_{\mathrm{lab}}\to Q_{\mathrm{com}})\right|
 359:  =\left|\frac{\sum_vK_v}{\bigcap_vK_v}\right|.
 360: \]
 361: 
 362: The Ext clause also marks a limitation of the homological image.  If
 363: \(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)=0\) and
 364: \(\operatorname{Ext}^1(H_1(N,\mathbb Z),A)\ne0\), a nonzero gerbe class can
 365: have \(K_\omega=0\) and \(Q_\omega=A\).  For instance, an
 366: \(\mathbb RP^2\)-type nerve and suitable \(2\)-torsion coefficients exhibit
 367: this behavior.  Conversely, when \(H_1(N,\mathbb Z)\) is free, the Ext term
 368: vanishes and zero evaluation forces \(\omega=0\).  These are UCT boundary
 369: cases, not additional classification results.

 succeeded in 282ms:
   1: \section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
   2: 
   3: A stack in groupoids has a sheaf of connected components.  A global section
   4: of that sheaf need not be represented by a global object of the stack, but it
   5: does select a maximal subgerbe.  This is Giraud's component-gerbe construction
   6: \cite[III, Prop.~2.1.5.3]{Giraud1971}.  The component is represented by a
   7: global object precisely when its gerbe is neutral, or equivalently when its
   8: Giraud \(H^2\)-class vanishes.  These classical facts are the starting point
   9: of the article.
  10: 
  11: Our setting is deliberately finite and presentation-relative.  We fix a site
  12: with terminal object, a presheaf \(F\), an abelian-banded prestack lift
  13: \(\mathfrak P\), and its stackification \(\mathfrak L\).  The connected
  14: components of \(\mathfrak P\) recover \(F\), while those of
  15: \(\mathfrak L\) recover the sheafification \(aF\).  If \(F\) is separated on
  16: the terminal slice and stackification is essentially surjective on the
  17: terminal fibre, a strict matching family without an amalgamation occurs
  18: exactly when \((aF)(a)\) is nonempty and every component gerbe selected by a
  19: section of \((aF)(a)\) is non-neutral.  This is a comparison between a strict
  20: presheaf predicate and standard gerbe theory, not a new classification of
  21: gerbes.
  22: 
  23: The first paper-specific point is a rigidity statement for a particularly
  24: strong kind of prestack presentation.  Suppose one selects a representative
  25: of every presheaf component and requires those representatives to be stable
  26: under pullback as literal equalities with respect to a split cleavage.  Under
  27: slice separatedness, terminal essential surjectivity then forces the
  28: sheafification unit
  29: \[
  30:  F(a)\longrightarrow(aF)(a)
  31: \]
  32: to be surjective.  Conversely, when \(H^1\) of the band vanishes,
  33: surjectivity makes the Isom torsor between a selected representative and an
  34: arbitrary terminal stack object trivial.  The result is a three-way
  35: equivalence between terminal essential surjectivity, bijectivity of the
  36: terminal sheafification unit, and the sheaf gluing condition at the terminal
  37: object.  The proof in \Cref{thm:canonical-lift-rigidity} distinguishes the
  38: strict-representative step from the classical \(H^1\)-torsor step.
  39: 
  40: The second point is an explicit finite-label prestack packaging.  Let
  41: \(\mathcal U\) be a finite good cover with connected nerve \(N\), let \(A\)
  42: be a finite abelian group, assume \(H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0\), and prescribe a
  43: finite family
  44: \[
  45:  \phi_\lambda:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\longrightarrow A.
  46: \]
  47: UCT selects classes in \(H^2(N,A)\), and the standard Cech construction
  48: realizes each class by an \(A\)-banded gerbe
  49: \cite{Breen2006,Moerdijk2002}.  The construction in
  50: \Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction} assembles these gerbes
  51: into one prestack while controlling four features simultaneously:
  52: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  53: \item the presheaf and sheaf of component labels;
  54: \item terminal essential surjectivity of stackification;
  55: \item the exact set of neutral and non-neutral labels;
  56: \item the evaluation maps and their image subgroups.
  57: \end{enumerate}
  58: The full construction and all four verifications are part of the article.
  59: 
  60: There is a coefficient hypothesis which cannot be suppressed.  For an
  61: ordinary nerve to index the Cech cochain complex with one copy of \(A\) per
  62: simplex, every nonempty finite overlap must be connected and the coefficient
  63: trivializations must be coherent.  On a disconnected overlap \(U_J\), the
  64: section group is \(A^{\pi_0(U_J)}\), information which the ordinary nerve
  65: does not contain.  We impose the connected-overlap hypothesis explicitly in
  66: \eqref{eq:connected-overlap-hypothesis}.  The open-star covers used for the
  67: wedge application have contractible nonempty intersections and satisfy it.
  68: 
  69: We also separate the formal algebra from the realization.  For a class
  70: \(\omega\in H^2(N,A)\), put
  71: \[
  72:  K_\omega=\operatorname{Im}
  73:  \bigl(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}_\omega}A\bigr),
  74:  \qquad Q_\omega=A/K_\omega.
  75: \]
  76: UCT naturality, quotient initiality, character annihilators, and the Ext
  77: kernel are collected in one proposition.  For finitely many component
  78: classes, the two aggregate quotients are
  79: \[
  80:  A/\bigcap_vK_v,
  81:  \qquad A/\sum_vK_v,
  82: \]
  83: and their comparison is the elementary exact sequence
  84: \[
  85:  0\longrightarrow
  86:  \frac{\sum_vK_v}{\bigcap_vK_v}
  87:  \longrightarrow
  88:  A/\bigcap_vK_v
  89:  \longrightarrow
  90:  A/\sum_vK_v
  91:  \longrightarrow0.
  92: \]
  93: No independent information-theoretic claim is made for the cardinalities of
  94: these fibres.
  95: 
  96: The finite-group endpoint is correspondingly stated as a realization
  97: corollary.  On the all-subdivision open-star basis of a finite model of
  98: \(\bigvee_{j=1}^{\beta}S^2\), a nonzero finite abelian group \(G\) is
  99: realized as the comparison kernel for two complementary nonzero component
 100: images exactly when
 101: \[
 102:  d(G)\le2\beta
 103:  \quad\text{and}\quad
 104:  G\text{ is not cyclic of prime-power order}.
 105: \]
 106: The proof combines the prescribed-image prestack with the elementary fact
 107: that the indecomposable finite abelian groups are the cyclic prime-power
 108: groups.  It is not a classification attached to an arbitrary space,
 109: presentation, or empirical model.
 110: 
 111: \subsection*{Inputs, standard steps, and paper-specific steps}
 112: 
 113: The logical order of the construction is
 114: \[
 115: \begin{array}{c}
 116: (\mathcal C,F,\mathfrak P,\mathscr A)\\
 117: \big\downarrow\text{\scriptsize stackification}\\
 118: \mathfrak L,\ \pi_0(\mathfrak L)\cong aF\\
 119: \big\downarrow\text{\scriptsize pull back over }v\in(aF)(a)\\
 120: \mathfrak G_v,\ [\mathfrak G_v]\in H^2(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\\
 121: \big\downarrow\text{\scriptsize selected finite presentation and UCT}\\
 122: \operatorname{ev}_{\omega_v}:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\to A,\quad
 123: K_v,\quad Q_v.
 124: \end{array}
 125: \]
 126: The first line is supplied data.  The component gerbe in the third line is
 127: Giraud's standard maximal subgerbe over a component section.  Its neutrality
 128: criterion and transport under a banded equivalence are also standard.  The
 129: last line uses standard Cech comparison and UCT after a finite presentation
 130: has been selected.
 131: 
 132: The article-specific statements concern restrictions and simultaneous
 133: control across this sequence.  The rigidity theorem identifies a strict
 134: representative hypothesis that collapses a possible terminal gluing failure
 135: when \(H^1\) vanishes.  The prescribed-realization theorem constructs one
 136: prestack in which the component presheaf, terminal fibre, neutrality set, and
 137: finite family of UCT evaluations are fixed together.  The wedge result is
 138: then a corollary obtained by combining that construction with finite-group
 139: arithmetic.  It is not presented as a separate classification mechanism.
 140: 
 141: This division also explains the theorem count.  Component-gerbe existence,
 142: neutrality, banded naturality, UCT, duality, subgroup sums and intersections,
 143: and quotient-fibre cardinality are consolidated rather than restated as
 144: independent theorems.  Numbered statements are retained only where they
 145: serve one of three roles:
 146: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
 147: \item record the standard component-gerbe package with its exact Giraud
 148:       antecedent;
 149: \item state a paper-specific comparison, rigidity, or simultaneous
 150:       realization assertion;
 151: \item isolate an application boundary or the wedge realization endpoint.
 152: \end{enumerate}
 153: 
 154: There are likewise two distinct uses of cohomology.  The Giraud class
 155: \[
 156:  [\mathfrak G_v]\in H^2(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)
 157: \]
 158: decides neutrality of the full component gerbe.  After a finite connected
 159: good-cover presentation with constant coefficient group \(A\) is fixed, its
 160: image under
 161: \[
 162:  H^2(N,A)\longrightarrow
 163:  \operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N,\mathbb Z),A)
 164: \]
 165: measures only evaluation on integral \(2\)-cycles.  A nonzero Ext class can
 166: be invisible to this map.  The homological image is therefore a quotient
 167: construction applied to a supplied gerbe class, not a complete obstruction
 168: theory for neutrality.
 169: 
 170: \subsection*{Scope and relation to the companion manuscript}
 171: 
 172: The lift data in this article are supplied.  They are not constructed from
 173: teams, logical models, or empirical probability tables.  A bare empirical
 174: model has a canonical split stack lift whose component gerbes are neutral.
 175: The Abramsky--Mansfield--Barbosa obstruction is instead a relative Cech
 176: \(H^1\)-torsor class indexed by a local section
 177: \cite{AbramskyMansfieldSoaresBarbosa2012,Caru2017}.  The empty-domain argument
 178: in \Cref{thm:empirical-model-boundary} prevents a universal recovery of those
 179: classes from a global-component-section map.
 180: 
 181: The companion manuscript
 182: \cite{MaZhang2026ConditionalGluingFailure} uses meet-semilattice sites.  The
 183: overlap with the present pullback-site treatment is as follows.
 184: \begin{center}
 185: \begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.31\textwidth}
 186:                 >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.27\textwidth}
 187:                 >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.30\textwidth}}
 188: \textbf{Topic here} & \textbf{Companion location} & \textbf{Status here}\\
 189: \hline
 190: Matching versus non-neutral components & Theorem 5.20 & Pullback-site
 191: reformulation with the dependency chain displayed\\
 192: Quotient initiality & Theorem 6.13 & Standard finite-nerve UCT clause in
 193: \Cref{prop:uct-aggregate-package}\\
 194: Component obstruction framework & Section 5 & Standard Giraud construction,
 195: cited at theorem level\\
 196: Terminal representative rigidity & No identical statement & Full narrow
 197: no-go proof in \Cref{thm:canonical-lift-rigidity}\\
 198: Finite-label prescribed realization & No identical package & Simultaneous
 199: control theorem in \Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction}\\
 200: \end{tabular}
 201: \end{center}
 202: The table identifies overlap rather than claiming separate priority for the
 203: first three rows.
 204: 
 205: \subsection*{Organization}
 206: 
 207: Section~\ref{sec:preliminaries} fixes the minimal presheaf and prestack
 208: interface.  Section~\ref{sec:gerbe-obstruction} records the standard Giraud
 209: component package, proves the comparison criterion, and gives the full
 210: terminal-rigidity proof.  Section~\ref{sec:homological-visibility} proves the
 211: connected-overlap, basis-site, and derived comparisons before constructing
 212: the prescribed finite-label prestack.  Section~\ref{sec:homological-images}
 213: collects the UCT and aggregate consequences.  The wedge application and the
 214: empirical-model boundary occupy Sections~\ref{sec:wedge-application} and
 215: \ref{sec:empirical-boundary}.  The separately compiled technical appendix
 216: contains only presentation bookkeeping, extended examples, and the narrow
 217: lower-language boundary argument; the proofs needed to certify the principal
 218: claims are all contained here.
