 succeeded in 270ms:
 263: We turn to the paper-specific packaging of the standard Cech-cocycle gerbe
 264: construction.  The point is not that a class in \(H^2\) can be represented
 265: by a gerbe; that is classical.  The content is simultaneous control of the
 266: component presheaf, terminal essential surjectivity, neutral and non-neutral
 267: labels, and the images of the evaluation homomorphisms.
 268: 
 269: \begin{theorem}[Finite-site realization of prescribed componentwise evaluation maps]
 270: \label{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction}
 271: Let \(\mathcal U=\{U_i\}_{i\in I}\) be a finite good cover satisfying
 272: \eqref{eq:connected-overlap-hypothesis}, with connected nerve \(N\), and let
 273: \(A\) be a finite abelian group.  Assume
 274: \[
 275:  H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0.
 276: \]
 277: Let \(\Lambda\) be a finite nonempty set and prescribe homomorphisms
 278: \[
 279:  \phi_\lambda:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\longrightarrow A
 280:  \qquad(\lambda\in\Lambda).
 281: \]
 282: Put \(\Lambda_0=\{\lambda:\phi_\lambda=0\}\).  Then there are a presheaf
 283: \(F\) on \(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}\) and an
 284: \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-banded prestack lift
 285: \(\mathfrak P\), with stackification \(\mathfrak L\), such that:
 286: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
 287: \item \(F(a)=\Lambda_0\), \((aF)(a)\cong\Lambda\), and
 288:       \(\pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)\cong\Lambda\);
 289: \item the component gerbe \(\mathfrak G_\lambda\) has class
 290:       \(\omega_\lambda\in H^2(N,A)\) satisfying
 291:       \[
 292:       \operatorname{ev}_{\omega_\lambda}=\phi_\lambda,
 293:       \qquad
 294:       \operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{ev}_{\omega_\lambda})
 295:       =\operatorname{Im}(\phi_\lambda),
 296:       \]
 297:       and \(\mathfrak G_\lambda\) is neutral exactly when
 298:       \(\lambda\in\Lambda_0\);
 299: \item \(\mathfrak P(a)\to\mathfrak L(a)\) is essentially surjective, and
 300:       its terminal objects occur exactly in the components indexed by
 301:       \(\Lambda_0\);
 302: \item \(F\) has a strict matching family on \(\mathcal U\), and
 303:       \[
 304:       \operatorname{MWA}(F,a)
 305:       \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 306:       \Lambda_0=\varnothing.
 307:       \]
 308: \end{enumerate}
 309: \end{theorem}
 310: 
 311: \begin{proof}
 312: The universal coefficient sequence and \(H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0\) give a natural
 313: isomorphism
 314: \begin{equation}\label{eq:uct-realization-isomorphism}
 315:  \eta_A:H^2(N,A)\xrightarrow{\sim}
 316:  \operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N,\mathbb Z),A).
 317: \end{equation}
 318: For every \(\lambda\), let \(\omega_\lambda\) be the inverse image of
 319: \(\phi_\lambda\).  Choose a normalized simplicial cocycle
 320: \(\alpha_\lambda\in Z^2(N,A)\) representing it, choosing
 321: \(\alpha_\lambda=0\) when \(\lambda\in\Lambda_0\).  Extend it alternately
 322: to ordered triples and set it to zero on triples with a repeated index.
 323: 
 324: The choice \(\alpha_\lambda=0\) for a zero map is legitimate because
 325: \(\eta_A\) is injective: \(\phi_\lambda=0\) implies
 326: \(\omega_\lambda=0\).  Conversely, a nonzero prescribed map has a nonzero
 327: class.  For pairwise distinct indices, alternating extension means
 328: \[
 329:  \alpha_{\lambda,\sigma(i)\sigma(j)\sigma(k)}
 330:  =\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)\alpha_{\lambda,ijk}.
 331: \]
 332: On every ordered \(3\)-simplex, the cocycle equation is
 333: \begin{equation}\label{eq:realization-cocycle-equation}
 334:  \alpha_{\lambda,jkl}-\alpha_{\lambda,ikl}
 335:  +\alpha_{\lambda,ijl}-\alpha_{\lambda,ijk}=0.
 336: \end{equation}
 337: These are the only coefficient identities used in the groupoid
 338: construction.
 339: 
 340: Define the component presheaf by
 341: \[
 342:  F(W)=
 343:  \begin{cases}
 344:  \Lambda,&W\ne a,\varnothing\text{ is a nonempty intersection object},\\
 345:  \Lambda_0,&W=a,\\
 346:  \{*\},&W=\varnothing.
 347:  \end{cases}
 348: \]
 349: Restrictions among proper nonempty intersections are identities,
 350: restrictions from \(a\) are the inclusion
 351: \(\Lambda_0\hookrightarrow\Lambda\), and restrictions to
 352: \(\varnothing\) are unique.  A matching family on the vertex cover has a
 353: single label on every connected component of the nerve.  Since \(N\) is
 354: connected, its label is a unique element of \(\Lambda\).  Consequently
 355: \begin{equation}\label{eq:label-sheafification}
 356:  (aF)(a)\cong\Lambda.
 357: \end{equation}
 358: 
 359: Here is the sheafification calculation in detail.  A family
 360: \((\lambda_i)_{i\in I}\) with \(\lambda_i\in F(U_i)=\Lambda\) is matching
 361: exactly when
 362: \[
 363:  \lambda_i=\lambda_j
 364:  \quad\text{for every edge }\{i,j\}\in N.
 365: \]
 366: No condition is imposed by an empty overlap because both restrictions land
 367: in the singleton \(F(\varnothing)\).  Edgewise equality propagates along
 368: paths in the connected \(1\)-skeleton of \(N\), so every matching family is
 369: constant.  Conversely, each constant family is matching.  It determines a
 370: section of \(aF\) over \(a\), and distinct constants remain distinct after
 371: restriction because every nonempty cover member retains its label.
 372: 
 373: Every cover in the generated pretopology is obtained from the vertex cover
 374: by pullback and transitivity.  On a nonempty proper intersection, a generated
 375: cover contains an identity, so it neither creates nor identifies labels.
 376: It follows that the vertex-cover calculation computes all terminal sections
 377: of the sheafification.  On proper nonempty objects,
 378: \((aF)(W)=\Lambda\), and on the initial object it is a singleton.
 379: 
 380: We next construct the prestack and verify its structure rather than citing a
 381: separate realization result.  Fix \(\lambda\notin\Lambda_0\).  Put
 382: \(\mathfrak P_\lambda(a)=\varnothing\).  For a nonempty proper intersection
 383: \(W\), let
 384: \[
 385:  I(W)=\{i\in I:W\subseteq U_i\}.
 386: \]
 387: The groupoid \(\mathfrak P_\lambda(W)\) has objects
 388: \(x_i^{\lambda,W}\) for \(i\in I(W)\), and
 389: \[
 390:  \operatorname{Hom}(x_i^{\lambda,W},x_j^{\lambda,W})
 391:  =A_{\mathcal U}(W)\cong A.
 392: \]
 393: Writing a morphism as \((b,i,j)\), define
 394: \begin{equation}\label{eq:twisted-composition}
 395:  (c,j,k)\circ(b,i,j)
 396:  =(b+c+\alpha_{\lambda,ijk},i,k).
 397: \end{equation}
 398: Normalization supplies identities and inverses.  Explicitly,
 399: \[
 400:  1_{x_i^{\lambda,W}}=(0,i,i),
 401:  \qquad
 402:  (b,i,j)^{-1}=(-b,j,i),
 403: \]
 404: because every cocycle coordinate with a repeated index is zero.  For three
 405: composable arrows, the two parenthesizations are
 406: \[
 407: \begin{aligned}
 408:  (d,k,l)\circ\bigl((c,j,k)\circ(b,i,j)\bigr)
 409:  &=(b+c+d+\alpha_{\lambda,ijk}
 410:        +\alpha_{\lambda,ikl},i,l),\\
 411:  \bigl((d,k,l)\circ(c,j,k)\bigr)\circ(b,i,j)
 412:  &=(b+c+d+\alpha_{\lambda,jkl}
 413:        +\alpha_{\lambda,ijl},i,l).
 414: \end{aligned}
 415: \]
 416: They agree by \eqref{eq:realization-cocycle-equation}.  Associativity is
 417: therefore exactly the cocycle identity
 418: \[
 419:  \alpha_{\lambda,ijk}+\alpha_{\lambda,ikl}
 420:  =\alpha_{\lambda,jkl}+\alpha_{\lambda,ijl}.
 421: \]
 422: If \(V\subseteq W\), then \(I(W)\subseteq I(V)\); restriction keeps the
 423: object index and applies the sheaf restriction to the coefficient.  These
 424: functors compose strictly.  Over \(\varnothing\) take the terminal groupoid.
 425: 
 426: To verify the restriction assertion, write
 427: \(\rho_{VW}:A_{\mathcal U}(W)\to A_{\mathcal U}(V)\).  The functor sends
 428: \[
 429:  x_i^{\lambda,W}\longmapsto x_i^{\lambda,V},
 430:  \qquad

 succeeded in 263ms:
 431:  (b,i,j)\longmapsto(\rho_{VW}(b),i,j).
 432: \]
 433: On nonempty connected intersections, the coherent trivializations identify
 434: \(\rho_{VW}\) with the identity of \(A\).  Hence
 435: \[
 436:  \rho_{VW}(b+c+\alpha_{\lambda,ijk})
 437:  =\rho_{VW}(b)+\rho_{VW}(c)+\alpha_{\lambda,ijk},
 438: \]
 439: so restriction preserves \eqref{eq:twisted-composition}.  If
 440: \(Z\subseteq V\subseteq W\), the equality
 441: \(\rho_{ZV}\rho_{VW}=\rho_{ZW}\) gives literal equality of the restriction
 442: functors.  This is strict functoriality, not functoriality only up to a
 443: cartesian comparison arrow.
 444: 
 445: For fixed \(i,j\in I(W)\), the presheaf of arrows between the corresponding
 446: objects on the slice over \(W\) is
 447: \[
 448:  V\longmapsto A_{\mathcal U}(V).
 449: \]
 450: It is the restriction of the genuine coefficient sheaf, and hence is a
 451: sheaf.  This verifies morphism descent for every twisted summand.
 452: Translation in the coefficient coordinate identifies
 453: \[
 454:  \underline{\operatorname{Aut}}(x_i^{\lambda,W})
 455:  \cong A_{\mathcal U}|_{\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}/W}.
 456: \]
 457: Changing \(i\) transports this identification through an arrow
 458: \((b,i,j)\); abelianness makes the induced conjugation on \(A\) trivial.
 459: The identifications therefore constitute an \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-banding.
 460: 
 461: For \(\lambda\in\Lambda_0\), let \(\mathfrak P_\lambda\) be the neutral
 462: one-object \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-banded stack on every nonempty object and the
 463: terminal groupoid on \(\varnothing\).  This is a stack here, not merely a
 464: one-object prestack: on the slice over a nonempty formal intersection, every
 465: generated cover contains an identity, so the nonempty part of the topology
 466: is trivial; the empty cover forces value zero at \(\varnothing\).  Evaluation
 467: at the terminal slice object is exact, whence
 468: \(H^1(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}/W,A_{\mathcal U})=0\), and every object
 469: descent torsor is trivial.
 470: 
 471: Concretely, descent data for the distinguished local object on a cover
 472: \(\{V_r\to W\}\) are arrows represented by coefficients
 473: \(d_{rs}\in A_{\mathcal U}(V_{rs})\) satisfying
 474: \[
 475:  d_{rs}+d_{st}=d_{rt}.
 476: \]
 477: They form an \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-torsor cocycle.  The vanishing just noted
 478: provides coefficients \(e_r\in A_{\mathcal U}(V_r)\) with
 479: \(d_{rs}=e_s-e_r\).  Adjusting the local identifications by \(e_r\) makes
 480: them agree and yields the global object.  Morphism descent is the ordinary
 481: sheaf condition for \(A_{\mathcal U}\).  Thus the neutral summand is already
 482: a stack on every relevant slice.
 483: 
 484: Set
 485: \[
 486:  \mathfrak P=\coprod_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathfrak P_\lambda
 487: \]
 488: fibrewise on nonempty objects, with a shared terminal fibre over
 489: \(\varnothing\).  For two objects in a twisted fibre, the isomorphism
 490: presheaf on the relevant slice is the restriction of the genuine sheaf
 491: \(A_{\mathcal U}\); it is therefore a sheaf.  Automorphism sheaves are the
 492: same sheaf, so \(\mathfrak P\) is an \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-banded prestack.
 493: Every nonempty proper fibre has connected components indexed by \(\Lambda\),
 494: the terminal fibre has components \(\Lambda_0\), and the empty fibre has one
 495: component.  Thus
 496: \[
 497:  \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P)\cong F.
 498: \]
 499: Stackification and \eqref{eq:label-sheafification} prove (i).
 500: 
 501: There are two points in this coproduct verification.  First, over a nonempty
 502: object there are no arrows between different \(\lambda\)-summands, so
 503: isomorphism descent cannot change a label.  A matching object descent datum
 504: therefore lies in one summand on each connected part of a cover, and the
 505: connected nerve forces a single label.  Second, all summands restrict to the
 506: same terminal groupoid over \(\varnothing\).  This causes no identification
 507: on a nonempty overlap: the initial object has no arrow back to a nonempty
 508: object and its unique descent datum contributes no coefficient.
 509: 
 510: For a twisted summand, every two named objects are isomorphic, since their
 511: Hom set is the nonempty set \(A\).  Thus each such fibre is connected.
 512: The neutral summand has one object and is connected as well.  It follows
 513: objectwise that the component presheaf is exactly \(F\), including its
 514: restriction \(\Lambda_0\hookrightarrow\Lambda\) out of the terminal object.
 515: The standard compatibility of stackification with components gives
 516: \[
 517:  \pi_0(\mathfrak L)\cong aF,
 518: \]
 519: and the terminal component sections are the labels in \(\Lambda\).
 520: 
 521: On \(U_i\), choose \(x_i^{\lambda,U_i}\); on \(U_{ij}\), choose the arrow
 522: \((0,i,j)\).  By \eqref{eq:twisted-composition}, its triple-overlap defect is
 523: \[
 524:  (0,i,k)^{-1}(0,j,k)(0,i,j)
 525:  =\alpha_{\lambda,ijk}
 526: \]
 527: under the band identification.  The Cech representative of the component
 528: gerbe is therefore exactly \(\alpha_\lambda\).  The good-cover comparison
 529: in \Cref{prop:finite-site-comparison} identifies its Giraud class with
 530: \(\omega_\lambda\), and
 531: \eqref{eq:uct-realization-isomorphism} gives
 532: \(\operatorname{ev}_{\omega_\lambda}=\phi_\lambda\).  By the standard
 533: component-gerbe package, this gerbe is neutral exactly when its class is zero,
 534: equivalently when \(\phi_\lambda=0\).  This proves (ii).
 535: 
 536: To check the sign in the defect, use
 537: \[
 538:  (0,i,k)^{-1}=(0,k,i)
 539: \]
 540: and apply \eqref{eq:twisted-composition} twice.  Normalization cancels the
 541: terms with repeated indices, while alternating extension moves the remaining
 542: term to the ordered coordinate \(\alpha_{\lambda,ijk}\).  This is the same
 543: convention as \eqref{eq:giraud-triple-defect}.  Therefore the class obtained
 544: by stackification is not merely cohomologous to a class with the desired
 545: evaluation: its chosen vertex-cover representative is the initially
 546: selected cocycle \(\alpha_\lambda\).
 547: 
 548: The connected-overlap hypothesis enters here a second time.  It identifies
 549: the automorphism section on \(U_{ijk}\) with one element of \(A\), so the
 550: defect is an ordinary simplicial coordinate.  If \(U_{ijk}\) were
 551: disconnected, the same calculation would yield a tuple in
 552: \(A^{\pi_0(U_{ijk})}\), and the prescribed ordinary-nerve cocycle would not
 553: specify the entire defect.
 554: 
 555: For \(\lambda\in\Lambda_0\), the neutral summand is already a stack and its
 556: terminal object maps to the corresponding terminal object of
 557: \(\mathfrak L\).  If \(\lambda\notin\Lambda_0\), the component gerbe is
 558: non-neutral and therefore has no object over \(a\).  Hence every object of
 559: \(\mathfrak L(a)\) lies in a zero component and comes from
 560: \(\mathfrak P(a)\), proving (iii), including the vacuous case
 561: \(\Lambda_0=\varnothing\).
 562: 
 563: When \(\Lambda_0\ne\varnothing\), an object of
 564: \(\mathfrak L(a)\) has some component label
 565: \(\lambda\in\Lambda\).  The component-gerbe package identifies the full
 566: substack at that label with \(\mathfrak G_\lambda\).  If
 567: \(\lambda\notin\Lambda_0\), non-neutrality says that this full substack has
 568: no terminal object, so the assumed object cannot have that label.  If
 569: \(\lambda\in\Lambda_0\), the distinguished terminal object of the neutral
 570: summand of \(\mathfrak P\) maps to an object in the same component of
 571: \(\mathfrak L\).  Any other terminal object in that component is isomorphic
 572: to it because the neutral component is a gerbe.  This is precisely essential
 573: surjectivity, rather than equality of object sets.
 574: 
 575: Finally, assigning one fixed \(\lambda\in\Lambda\) to every \(U_i\) is a
 576: strict matching family: restrictions on nonempty overlaps are identities and
 577: those on empty overlaps are unique.  It has an amalgamation precisely when
 578: \(F(a)=\Lambda_0\) is nonempty.  This proves (iv) and completes the
 579: construction.
 580: 
 581: In terms of the definition from \Cref{sec:preliminaries}, the last
 582: equivalence uses only existence.  If \(\Lambda_0=\varnothing\), every
 583: constant label gives a strict matching family and there is no global
 584: section, so matching without amalgamation holds.  If
 585: \(\Lambda_0\ne\varnothing\), \(F(a)\) is nonempty and the predicate
 586: \(\operatorname{MWA}(F,a)\) is false, although matching families with
 587: nonzero labels may still fail individually to amalgamate.  The theorem does
 588: not claim that every matching family amalgamates in this case.
 589: \end{proof}
 590: 
 591: The gerbes in this proof are obtained from the standard Cech-cocycle
 592: construction.  What the theorem adds is the single finite-label prestack
 593: whose sheaf of components, terminal fibre, neutrality pattern, and homological
 594: images are all prescribed simultaneously.
 595: 
 596: \subsection*{The two terminal-fibre endpoints}
 597: 
 598: If every prescribed map is zero, then \(\Lambda_0=\Lambda\).  The component
 599: presheaf is already constant on every nonempty object, all summands are
 600: neutral, and the stackification has a terminal object in every global
 601: component.  The theorem reduces to a finite coproduct of split
 602: \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-banded stacks.  Matching families and global presheaf
 603: sections are both indexed by \(\Lambda\).
 604: 
 605: At the other endpoint, suppose every prescribed map is nonzero.  Then
 606: \(\Lambda_0=\varnothing\), so
 607: \[
 608:  F(a)=\varnothing,\qquad
 609:  (aF)(a)=\Lambda,\qquad
 610:  \mathfrak P(a)=\varnothing.

 succeeded in 275ms:
 611: \]
 612: Every global component gerbe is non-neutral, hence
 613: \(\mathfrak L(a)=\varnothing\) as well.  The map
 614: \[
 615:  \mathfrak P(a)\longrightarrow\mathfrak L(a)
 616: \]
 617: is essentially surjective because both fibres are empty.  This is the
 618: vacuous terminal-essential-surjectivity case used by matching without
 619: amalgamation.  It is not a claim that the non-neutral global component
 620: sections have terminal objects; they do not.
 621: 
 622: For a mixed family, \(\mathfrak L(a)\) contains objects exactly in the
 623: components indexed by \(\Lambda_0\).  The nonzero labels still occur as
 624: sections of \(\pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)\cong(aF)(a)\), and their full component
 625: substacks are locally inhabited gerbes without terminal objects.  This is
 626: the concrete distinction among the three terminal levels in
 627: \eqref{eq:three-terminal-levels}.
 628: 
 629: \subsection*{A three-label calculation}
 630: 
 631: Take a triangulated \(2\)-sphere and an open-star cover of a barycentric
 632: subdivision.  Its nerve \(N\) satisfies
 633: \[
 634:  H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0,\qquad H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z.
 635: \]
 636: Let \(A=\mathbb Z/6\mathbb Z\), and take the label set
 637: \[
 638:  \Lambda=\{\mathtt n,\mathtt p,\mathtt q\}.
 639: \]
 640: Prescribe
 641: \[
 642: \begin{aligned}
 643:  \phi_{\mathtt n}(1)&=0,\\
 644:  \phi_{\mathtt p}(1)&=2,\\
 645:  \phi_{\mathtt q}(1)&=3.
 646: \end{aligned}
 647: \]
 648: Then
 649: \[
 650:  \Lambda_0=\{\mathtt n\},\qquad
 651:  K_{\mathtt n}=0,\qquad
 652:  K_{\mathtt p}=\langle2\rangle,\qquad
 653:  K_{\mathtt q}=\langle3\rangle.
 654: \]
 655: The three image groups have orders \(1,3,2\), respectively.  The UCT
 656: isomorphism chooses three classes in \(H^2(N,A)\cong A\), and normalized
 657: cocycles \(\alpha_{\mathtt n}=0\),
 658: \(\alpha_{\mathtt p}\), and \(\alpha_{\mathtt q}\) feed the twisted
 659: composition formula.
 660: 
 661: The component presheaf has
 662: \[
 663:  F(a)=\{\mathtt n\},
 664:  \qquad
 665:  F(U_J)=\{\mathtt n,\mathtt p,\mathtt q\}
 666: \]
 667: on every proper nonempty intersection.  Its sheafification has three
 668: terminal component sections.  Only the \(\mathtt n\)-component has a
 669: terminal stack object.  Therefore
 670: \[
 671:  \pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)
 672:  =\{\mathtt n,\mathtt p,\mathtt q\},
 673:  \qquad
 674:  \pi_0(\mathfrak L(a))=\{\mathtt n\}.
 675: \]
 676: The notation on the right means the set of isomorphism classes of actual
 677: terminal objects; it is smaller than the value at \(a\) of the component
 678: sheaf.
 679: 
 680: For the two nonzero labels,
 681: \[
 682:  Q_{\mathtt p}=A/\langle2\rangle\cong\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z,
 683:  \qquad
 684:  Q_{\mathtt q}=A/\langle3\rangle\cong\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z.
 685: \]
 686: Their images are complementary:
 687: \[
 688:  \langle2\rangle\cap\langle3\rangle=0,
 689:  \qquad
 690:  \langle2\rangle+\langle3\rangle=A.
 691: \]
 692: If all three labels are aggregated, the zero image from
 693: \(\mathtt n\) keeps the intersection equal to zero, while the sum remains
 694: \(A\).  Hence
 695: \[
 696:  Q_{\mathrm{lab}}=A,\qquad Q_{\mathrm{com}}=0.
 697: \]
 698: 
 699: This mixed example does not satisfy matching without amalgamation because
 700: \(F(a)\ne\varnothing\).  It does contain matching families with labels
 701: \(\mathtt p\) and \(\mathtt q\) which individually have no amalgamation.
 702: Deleting the neutral label produces the all-nonzero endpoint above: the same
 703: two component classes remain, \(F(a)\) becomes empty, and the existence
 704: predicate \(\operatorname{MWA}(F,a)\) becomes true.

 succeeded in 249ms:
   1: \section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
   2: 
   3: The article starts with supplied finite-site data: a presheaf, an
   4: abelian-banded prestack lift, and its stackification.  Giraud's component
   5: construction then assigns the maximal component gerbe to each global section
   6: of the component sheaf.  The use made of this standard input is narrow: under
   7: slice separatedness and terminal essential surjectivity it compares the
   8: strict presheaf predicate of matching without amalgamation with non-neutrality
   9: of every global component.  Representative-rigidity shows why a strictly
  10: pullback-stable choice of lift representatives is too restrictive in the
  11: basic \(H^1\)-vanishing regime.
  12: 
  13: On a finite good-cover site, the connected-overlap hypothesis is what permits
  14: the ordinary nerve to carry the full constant-coefficient Cech complex.  The
  15: all-subdivision basis comparison identifies the generated site with the
  16: ordinary sheaf theory of the underlying polyhedron.  Within this fixed
  17: setting, the standard Cech-cocycle gerbe construction can be packaged into a
  18: single prestack whose component presheaf, terminal behavior, neutrality
  19: pattern, and evaluation images are prescribed simultaneously.
  20: 
  21: UCT and elementary subgroup arithmetic then yield the aggregate quotients.
  22: For the selected open-star covers of
  23: \(\bigvee_{j=1}^{\beta}S^2\), the two-component realization criterion is
  24: \[
  25:  d(G)\le2\beta
  26:  \quad\text{and}\quad
  27:  G\text{ is not cyclic of prime-power order}.
  28: \]
  29: This is a sharp corollary of the selected realization, not a new
  30: classification principle for finite abelian groups or gerbes.
  31: 
  32: Bare empirical models provide only the support presheaf and its canonical
  33: split stack, whose component gerbes are neutral.  Their relative degree-one
  34: obstructions are indexed differently and are not recovered by the
  35: component-section map.  A non-split banded lift must therefore be supplied
  36: before the finite-site results apply.
  37: 
  38: The resulting contribution has a definite boundary.  The following parts
  39: are classical inputs:
  40: \[
  41: \begin{array}{ll}
  42: \text{component over }v\text{ is a gerbe}
  43:  &\text{Giraud III.2.1.5.3},\\
  44: \text{neutrality}\Longleftrightarrow[\mathfrak G_v]=0
  45:  &\text{abelian-banded gerbe classification},\\
  46: \text{finite Cech class}\longrightarrow\text{UCT evaluation}
  47:  &\text{Leray comparison and UCT},\\
  48: \text{aggregate exact sequence}
  49:  &\text{subgroup and quotient arithmetic.}
  50: \end{array}
  51: \]
  52: They are stated together so that the hypotheses used by the applications
  53: remain visible, not as separate advances.
  54: 
  55: The two proofs requiring construction beyond those inputs are terminal
  56: rigidity and prescribed simultaneous realization.  The first identifies
  57: where literal pullback stability removes a descent cocycle and where
  58: \(H^1\)-vanishing removes the remaining Isom torsor.  The second constructs
  59: the label presheaf and twisted groupoid fibres, verifies strict restrictions
  60: and the genuine constant band, computes stackification components and
  61: terminal objects, and recovers the selected Cech cocycles.  Their full proofs
  62: make the claimed scope independently checkable within the article.
  63: 
  64: The technical appendix has a limited role.  Its presentation groupoid records
  65: naturality only along specified comparison words and does not assert path
  66: independence.  Its finite computations illustrate the UCT and subgroup
  67: formulas.  Its lower-language example explains why the finite-site lift is
  68: treated as input but does not construct that lift.
  69: 
  70: No crossed-module comparison is pursued: the present arguments begin after a
  71: gerbe \(H^2\)-class and its Cech representative are supplied, and contain
  72: neither the source-side degree-three chain model nor the comparison cochain
  73: required to derive such a class from crossed-module data.

 succeeded in 268ms:
   1: \section{Homological images and aggregate quotients}
   2: \label{sec:homological-images}
   3: 
   4: Fix a finite simplicial complex \(N\), a finite abelian group \(A\), and a
   5: class \(\omega\in H^2(N,A)\).  The universal coefficient map is denoted by
   6: \[
   7:  \operatorname{ev}_\omega:
   8:  H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\longrightarrow A.
   9: \]
  10: Define its \emph{homological image} and \emph{homological cokernel} by
  11: \begin{equation}\label{eq:homological-image-cokernel}
  12:  K_\omega:=\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{ev}_\omega),
  13:  \qquad Q_\omega:=A/K_\omega.
  14: \end{equation}
  15: These definitions depend on the supplied finite presentation of the component
  16: gerbe.  Refinement and retrivialization transport them by UCT naturality, but
  17: no canonical identification along distinct comparison zigzags is asserted.
  18: The resulting freely generated presentation groupoid and its elementary
  19: naturality calculation are recorded in the technical appendix rather than
  20: promoted to an invariance theorem.
  21: 
  22: For a finite family of component classes
  23: \((\omega_v)_{v\in V}\), put \(K_v=K_{\omega_v}\) and define
  24: \begin{equation}\label{eq:aggregate-quotients}
  25:  Q_{\mathrm{lab}}:=A\Big/\bigcap_{v\in V}K_v,
  26:  \qquad
  27:  Q_{\mathrm{com}}:=A\Big/\sum_{v\in V}K_v.
  28: \end{equation}
  29: The first quotient retains enough information to map to every componentwise
  30: quotient \(A/K_v\).  The second retains only characters which descend through
  31: every one of them.
  32: 
  33: \begin{proposition}[UCT and aggregate subgroup package]
  34: \label{prop:uct-aggregate-package}
  35: With the notation above:
  36: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  37: \item The universal coefficient sequence is
  38:       \[
  39:       0\longrightarrow
  40:       \operatorname{Ext}^1(H_1(N,\mathbb Z),A)
  41:       \longrightarrow H^2(N,A)
  42:       \xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}}
  43:       \operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N,\mathbb Z),A)
  44:       \longrightarrow0.
  45:       \]
  46:       Thus \(K_\omega=0\) exactly when \(\omega\) lies in the displayed
  47:       Ext subgroup.  In particular, nonzero classes can have zero
  48:       homological image when \(H_1\) has torsion.
  49: \item The quotient map \(q_\omega:A\to Q_\omega\) is initial among group
  50:       homomorphisms \(q:A\to B\) satisfying
  51:       \(q\circ\operatorname{ev}_\omega=0\).  A character
  52:       \(\chi:A\to\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z\) factors through \(Q_\omega\)
  53:       exactly when \(\chi(K_\omega)=0\).  Since finite Pontryagin duality
  54:       separates points,
  55:       \[
  56:       K_\omega=\mathop{\bigcap}_{\chi(K_\omega)=0}\ker\chi.
  57:       \]
  58: \item For a nonempty finite family \((K_v)_{v\in V}\), there is a natural
  59:       short exact sequence
  60:       \begin{equation}\label{eq:aggregate-exact-sequence}
  61:       0\longrightarrow
  62:       \frac{\sum_vK_v}{\bigcap_vK_v}
  63:       \longrightarrow Q_{\mathrm{lab}}
  64:       \longrightarrow Q_{\mathrm{com}}
  65:       \longrightarrow0.
  66:       \end{equation}
  67:       Moreover,
  68:       \[
  69:       \widehat{Q_{\mathrm{com}}}
  70:       \cong\bigcap_{v\in V}K_v^\perp,
  71:       \qquad
  72:       \widehat{Q_{\mathrm{lab}}}
  73:       \cong\sum_{v\in V}K_v^\perp
  74:       \]
  75:       inside \(\widehat A\).
  76: \item If \(H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0\) and
  77:       \(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta\), a subgroup
  78:       \(K\le A\) occurs as \(K_\omega\) exactly when \(d(K)\le\beta\).
  79:       Every finite family of such subgroups is simultaneously attained by
  80:       the prestack of
  81:       \Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction}.
  82: \end{enumerate}
  83: \end{proposition}
  84: 
  85: \begin{proof}
  86: Clause (i) is the cohomological universal coefficient theorem, natural in
  87: both \(N\) and \(A\) \cite[Sec.~3.1]{Hatcher2002}.  Its middle-to-right map
  88: evaluates a cohomology class on integral \(2\)-cycles, so its value at
  89: \(\omega\) is \(\operatorname{ev}_\omega\).  Exactness identifies the
  90: kernel with the Ext term.
  91: 
  92: For a simplicial map \(f:N'\to N\), naturality is the commutative square
  93: \[
  94: \begin{CD}
  95: H^2(N,A) @>{\operatorname{ev}}>>
  96:  \operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N,\mathbb Z),A)\\
  97: @V{f^*}VV @VV{(-)\circ f_*}V\\
  98: H^2(N',A) @>{\operatorname{ev}}>>
  99:  \operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N',\mathbb Z),A).
 100: \end{CD}
 101: \]
 102: Thus
 103: \[
 104:  \operatorname{ev}_{f^*\omega}
 105:  =\operatorname{ev}_\omega\circ f_*.
 106: \]
 107: For a coefficient homomorphism \(\theta:A\to A'\), the corresponding
 108: identity is
 109: \[
 110:  \operatorname{ev}_{\theta_*\omega}
 111:  =\theta\circ\operatorname{ev}_\omega.
 112: \]
 113: These formulas are the only invariance input used for a specified
 114: presentation comparison.  The short exact UCT sequence may split as a
 115: sequence of groups, but no natural splitting is chosen.
 116: 
 117: For (ii), \(q\circ\operatorname{ev}_\omega=0\) says precisely that \(q\)
 118: vanishes on \(K_\omega\).  The universal property of the quotient gives a
 119: unique factorization through \(A/K_\omega\).  Apply the same observation to
 120: characters.  The intersection formula follows because characters of a
 121: finite abelian group separate points \cite{Terras1999}.
 122: 
 123: For the last assertion, pass to the finite quotient \(A/K_\omega\).  If
 124: \(a\notin K_\omega\), then \(\bar a\ne0\) in that quotient.  Finite
 125: Pontryagin duality gives a character
 126: \[
 127:  \bar\chi:A/K_\omega\longrightarrow\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z
 128: \]
 129: with \(\bar\chi(\bar a)\ne0\).  Its pullback \(\chi=\bar\chi q_\omega\)
 130: annihilates \(K_\omega\) but not \(a\).  Hence no element outside
 131: \(K_\omega\) belongs to the displayed intersection of kernels.  The reverse
 132: containment is immediate.
 133: 
 134: The containment \(\bigcap_vK_v\subseteq\sum_vK_v\) gives the quotient map in
 135: \eqref{eq:aggregate-exact-sequence}.  By the third isomorphism theorem its
 136: kernel is
 137: \[
 138:  \frac{\sum_vK_v}{\bigcap_vK_v},
 139: \]
 140: which proves exactness.  For finite abelian groups, annihilators exchange sums and
 141: intersections:
 142: \[
 143:  \Big(\sum_vK_v\Big)^\perp=\bigcap_vK_v^\perp,
 144:  \qquad
 145:  \Big(\bigcap_vK_v\Big)^\perp=\sum_vK_v^\perp.
 146: \]
 147: Dualizing the two quotients proves the character formulas in (iii).
 148: 
 149: The first annihilator identity does not require finiteness:
 150: \(\chi\) kills \(\sum_vK_v\) exactly when it kills every \(K_v\).  For the
 151: second, one inclusion is immediate:
 152: \[
 153:  \sum_vK_v^\perp\subseteq\Big(\bigcap_vK_v\Big)^\perp.
 154: \]
 155: For finite groups, equality follows from exactness of Pontryagin duality.
 156: It may also be checked by cardinalities, using
 157: \[
 158:  |H^\perp|=\frac{|A|}{|H|}
 159: \]
 160: and, for two subgroups,
 161: \[
 162:  |K+L|\,|K\cap L|=|K|\,|L|.
 163: \]
 164: Induction gives the formula for a finite family.
 165: 
 166: Under the hypotheses of (iv), the Ext term vanishes and UCT becomes
 167: \[
 168:  H^2(N,A)\cong\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb Z^\beta,A).
 169: \]
 170: The image of a homomorphism from \(\mathbb Z^\beta\) is generated by the
 171: images of the standard basis, so it has at most \(\beta\) generators.
 172: Conversely, given generators \(k_1,\dots,k_\beta\) of \(K\), allowing zeros
 173: when fewer are needed, the map
 174: \[
 175:  (n_1,\dots,n_\beta)\longmapsto\sum_jn_jk_j
 176: \]
 177: has image \(K\).  Applying
 178: \Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction} to a family of these maps
 179: gives simultaneous realization.
 180: 
 181: The number \(d(K)\) can be read prime by prime.  If
 182: \[
 183:  K\cong\bigoplus_p\bigoplus_{j=1}^{r_p}C_{p^{e_{p,j}}},
 184: \]
 185: then
 186: \[
 187:  d(K)=\max_p r_p.
 188: \]
 189: Thus the condition in (iv) is not a bound on \(|K|\) or on its exponent.
 190: It is precisely the rank bound imposed by the \(\beta\) basis elements of
 191: \(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\).  Once homomorphisms with the desired images have been
 192: chosen componentwise, simultaneous attainment is the prestack-level part
 193: supplied by \Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction}.
 194: \end{proof}
 195: 

 succeeded in 276ms:
 190: 
 191: The criterion is a site-theoretic reformulation of the meet-semilattice
 192: comparison in the companion manuscript
 193: \cite[Theorem~5.20]{MaZhang2026ConditionalGluingFailure}; it is not an
 194: independent gerbe-classification theorem.  Its role here is to connect the
 195: strict presheaf predicate to the standard component-gerbe construction on a
 196: pullback site.
 197: 
 198: We now give the representative-rigidity argument in full.  This result
 199: isolates a restriction on attempts to realize matching without amalgamation
 200: by an excessively strict lift.
 201: 
 202: \begin{theorem}[Terminal rigidity for representative-rigid lifts]
 203: \label{thm:canonical-lift-rigidity}
 204: Let \(F\) be separated on the slice over \(a\), and let \(\mathfrak P\) be a
 205: representative-rigid \(\mathscr A\)-banded prestack lift of \(F\).  Write
 206: \(u:F\to aF\) and \(\iota:\mathfrak P\to\mathfrak L=a(\mathfrak P)\).
 207: Then:
 208: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
 209: \item terminal essential surjectivity of \(\iota\) implies that
 210:       \(u_a:F(a)\to(aF)(a)\) is surjective;
 211: \item if \(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)=0\), surjectivity of \(u_a\) implies
 212:       terminal essential surjectivity of \(\iota\).
 213: \end{enumerate}
 214: Under the same \(H^1\)-vanishing hypothesis, the following are equivalent:
 215: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),nosep]
 216: \item \(\iota\) is terminally essentially surjective;
 217: \item \(u_a\) is bijective;
 218: \item every strict matching family on every cover of \(a\) has a unique
 219:       amalgamation in \(F(a)\).
 220: \end{enumerate}
 221: Strict pullback stability of the selected representatives is used in
 222: \textup{(i)}.  The converse \textup{(ii)} uses only the standard
 223: \(\mathscr A\)-torsor of isomorphisms and its \(H^1\)-class.
 224: \end{theorem}
 225: 
 226: \begin{proof}
 227: We separate the proof into the forward descent construction, the converse
 228: torsor argument, and the comparison with strict amalgamation.
 229: 
 230: \medskip
 231: \noindent\emph{Forward descent construction.}
 232: Let
 233: \(\xi\in(aF)(a)=\pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)\).  By the proof of
 234: \Cref{prop:finite-site-semantic-interface}(i), there is a cover
 235: \(\{U_i\to a\}\) and a strict matching family \(t_i\in F(U_i)\)
 236: representing \(\xi\).  Representative-rigidity supplies objects
 237: \[
 238:  y_i=s_{U_i}(t_i)\in\mathfrak P(U_i).
 239: \]
 240: On \(U_{ij}\), strict pullback stability gives literal equalities
 241: \[
 242:  y_i|_{U_{ij}}
 243:  =s_{U_{ij}}(t_i|_{U_{ij}})
 244:  =s_{U_{ij}}(t_j|_{U_{ij}})
 245:  =y_j|_{U_{ij}}.
 246: \]
 247: The identity arrows of these common objects are therefore legitimate
 248: descent arrows.  Their triple composites are identities on the nose, so
 249: stack descent glues the \(y_i\) to an object
 250: \(x_\xi\in\mathfrak L(a)\) with component class \(\xi\).  This is the sole
 251: place where strict pullback stability is used.  With merely
 252: pseudofunctorial representatives, cartesian comparison arrows would enter
 253: and their coherence would have to be controlled separately.
 254: 
 255: The descent datum just constructed is stronger than the datum provided by
 256: equality of component classes alone.  Equality
 257: \([y_i|_{U_{ij}}]=[y_j|_{U_{ij}}]\) would only guarantee local
 258: isomorphisms after further covers.  Equation
 259: \eqref{eq:strict-representative-pullback} instead gives the same object on
 260: the overlap.  Hence the chosen descent arrow is the identity, not a selected
 261: arrow in a nontrivial Isom torsor.  On \(U_{ijk}\) the descent equation is
 262: \[
 263:  1_{y_k}\circ1_{y_j}=1_{y_i}
 264: \]
 265: after the literal identifications of the three restrictions.  No
 266: \(\mathscr A\)-valued \(2\)-cocycle remains to be killed.  This explains
 267: exactly why strict pullback stability, rather than a componentwise choice of
 268: objects, is the forward hypothesis.
 269: 
 270: Assume terminal essential surjectivity.  There is
 271: \(y\in\mathfrak P(a)\) and an isomorphism
 272: \(\iota(y)\cong x_\xi\).  Let \(t\in F(a)\) be the component of \(y\).
 273: The component map of \(\iota\) is \(u\), hence
 274: \[
 275:  u_a(t)=[\iota(y)]=[x_\xi]=\xi.
 276: \]
 277: Since \(\xi\) was arbitrary, \(u_a\) is surjective.  This proves (i).
 278: 
 279: \medskip
 280: \noindent\emph{The converse Isom-torsor argument.}
 281: For (ii), assume \(u_a\) is surjective and take any
 282: \(x\in\mathfrak L(a)\).  Choose \(t\in F(a)\) with
 283: \(u_a(t)=[x]\), and put \(y=s_a(t)\).  The sheaf
 284: \[
 285:  \mathcal I_{y,x}:=
 286:  \underline{\operatorname{Isom}}_{\mathfrak L}(\iota(y),x)
 287: \]
 288: is locally nonempty because the two objects have the same component class.
 289: Composition makes it a simply transitive torsor under
 290: \(\underline{\operatorname{Aut}}(\iota(y))\cong\mathscr A\).  This is the
 291: usual Isom-torsor of a banded stack
 292: \cite[Chap.~III]{MacLaneMoerdijk1994}, \cite[Tag 04TU]{StacksProject}.
 293: More explicitly, choose a cover \(\{V_j\to a\}\) and isomorphisms
 294: \[
 295:  h_j:\iota(y)|_{V_j}\xrightarrow{\sim}x|_{V_j}.
 296: \]
 297: On \(V_{jk}\), the ratio
 298: \[
 299:  d_{jk}=h_k^{-1}h_j
 300: \]
 301: is an automorphism of \(\iota(y)|_{V_{jk}}\), hence a section of
 302: \(\mathscr A(V_{jk})\).  The equality of threefold composites gives
 303: \[
 304:  d_{jk}+d_{kl}=d_{jl}
 305: \]
 306: on \(V_{jkl}\), so \(d=(d_{jk})\) is a Cech \(1\)-cocycle representing
 307: the Isom torsor.  If its class vanishes, there are sections
 308: \(e_j\in\mathscr A(V_j)\) with \(d_{jk}=e_k-e_j\).  Replacing
 309: \(h_j\) by \(h_j\cdot e_j\) makes the local isomorphisms agree on overlaps,
 310: and the sheaf condition for Isom glues them to a global isomorphism
 311: \(\iota(y)\cong x\).
 312: 
 313: The hypothesis \(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)=0\) makes every such torsor
 314: trivial, so \(\mathcal I_{y,x}\) has a global section.  Thus
 315: \(\iota(y)\cong x\), proving terminal essential surjectivity.
 316: 
 317: \medskip
 318: \noindent\emph{Comparison with strict amalgamation.}
 319: It remains to compare (b) and (c).  Slice separatedness implies that \(u_a\)
 320: is injective: equality after sheafification is witnessed after a cover, and
 321: separatedness at \(a\) returns equality in \(F(a)\).  Thus surjectivity and
 322: bijectivity of \(u_a\) are equivalent.
 323: 
 324: Suppose \(u_a\) is bijective and let \((t_i)\) be a strict matching family
 325: on a cover of \(a\).  It glues in \(aF\) to \(\xi\); choose
 326: \(t\in F(a)\) with \(u_a(t)=\xi\).  Over each \(U_i\), the sections
 327: \(t|_{U_i}\) and \(t_i\) become equal in \(aF(U_i)\).  Such equality is
 328: witnessed after a further cover of \(U_i\), and separatedness at that object
 329: of the slice gives \(t|_{U_i}=t_i\).  Hence \(t\) is an amalgamation, unique
 330: by separatedness at \(a\).
 331: 
 332: Conversely, assume every matching family on a cover of \(a\) has a unique
 333: amalgamation.  Given \(\xi\in(aF)(a)\), the plus--plus representation and
 334: slice separatedness used in
 335: \Cref{prop:finite-site-semantic-interface}(i) produce a strict matching
 336: family representing \(\xi\).  Its amalgamation \(t\in F(a)\) satisfies
 337: \(u_a(t)=\xi\), so \(u_a\) is surjective and hence bijective.  Combining
 338: this equivalence with (i) and (ii) proves (a)--(c).
 339: 
 340: The roles of the hypotheses are now separate.  Slice separatedness upgrades
 341: locally witnessed equality of presheaf sections to strict equality and gives
 342: uniqueness of amalgamations.  Representative-rigidity turns a strict
 343: matching family into identity-valued object descent data.  Terminal
 344: essential surjectivity is used only in the forward passage from a stack
 345: object to a prestack object.  Finally,
 346: \(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)=0\) is used only to trivialize the Isom torsor
 347: in the converse.  None of these steps supplies a new classification of
 348: gerbes.
 349: \end{proof}
 350: 
 351: Consequently, on a slice with \(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)=0\), matching
 352: without amalgamation cannot coexist with both terminal essential
 353: surjectivity and a strictly pullback-stable representative choice.  This is
 354: a no-go statement for that rigid presentation of a lift; it is not a new
 355: general descent or gerbe-classification principle.

codex
The key construction can be made honest without choosing a refinement zigzag. On each cover, the twisted groupoids use \(\Gamma(U_J,\mathcal A)\); a gauge \(c\) acts by subtracting \(c_{ij}\) from arrow coordinates, a \(0\)-cochain gives the corresponding natural transformation, and a refinement sends a chart index \(j\) to its assigned index \(r(j)\). These formulas compose strictly; the localization’s hom-groupoids then have \(\pi_0=H^1\) and automorphism group \(H^0\), which is exactly the unavoidable ambiguity rather than a hidden choice.

# Reproducing the finite and symbolic checks

Run all commands from the paper directory. The stored run used Python 3.10.11.
The verifier and tests use only the Python standard library.

## Verification commands

```sh
python -m unittest discover -s artifacts -p "test_*.py" -v
python artifacts/verify_A9_r1.py --output artifacts/verify_A9_r1_output.txt
```

The unit suite prints `Ran 5 tests` followed by `OK`. The archived report begins
with `PASS NWW Cechization formula checks` and ends with:

```text
OPEN comparisons intentionally unverified: NWW Problems 8.1(b), 8.2(a), 8.2(b)
```

The verifier exits zero. Its counterexample line is an expected finite check,
not a verification failure. The report uses Unix LF and contains no clock or
date fields. The separate priority audit is recorded in
`artifacts/literature_check.md`.

## Checksum verification

Paths in `SHA256SUMS` are relative to the `artifacts` directory. From the paper
directory, run exactly:

```sh
cd artifacts && sha256sum -c SHA256SUMS
```

A successful check prints exactly 6 `OK` lines and no failures. The
project-local `.gitattributes` pins every file covered by the manifest to LF.

  60: | The matching/non-neutrality equivalence | A paper-specific corollary obtained by matching separatedness, sheafification, terminal essential surjectivity, and neutrality; not a new gerbe obstruction theorem, and substantially present in the cited companion project |
  61: | Homological images, Ext-kernel descriptions, annihilating-character descriptions, and quotient factorization | Formal consequences of UCT naturality, finite duality, and quotient universal properties |
  62: | Intersection/sum quotients and their exact sequence | Elementary subgroup and quotient arithmetic |
  63: | Unrestricted auxiliary-register sizes | Fibre cardinality of quotient maps, not an independent information-theoretic result |
  64: | The finite-abelian decomposition used in the wedge result | Standard finite-abelian group structure and generator arithmetic |
  65: | The split component stack used in the empirical-model comparison | Standard construction |
  66: 
  67: Accordingly, whenever this manuscript applies one of these facts in its chosen
  68: finite-site notation, that application should be described as an application
  69: of a standard result. New notation, a named quotient, or a theorem-sized
  70: restatement does not by itself establish mathematical priority.
  71: 
  72: ## Two-label wedge classification
  73: 
  74: The referee found no previously published theorem stating the entire
  75: two-label wedge classification verbatim. That negative search result must be
  76: recorded together with its qualification: it does not support a substantial
  77: priority claim. An exact statement obtained by composing standard
  78: equivalences can be formally new while carrying little independent
  79: mathematical priority.
  80: 
  81: Here the wedge statement is derived from `H_2` of a wedge of 2-spheres, the
  82: description of attainable images of maps from a free abelian group, the
  83: equivalence between complementary image subgroups and an internal direct-sum
  84: decomposition, the classification of finite indecomposable abelian groups,
  85: and generator bounds on the two summands. The resulting exact criterion may
  86: be specific to this selected realization problem, but it is an elementary
  87: two-component realization corollary, not a new classification theorem about
  88: finite abelian groups or gerbes. It is also presentation-relative to the
  89: chosen good-cover construction.
  90: 
  91: ## Closed route: Neeb-Wagemann-Wockel comparison
  92: 
  93: The bibliography records Karl-Hermann Neeb, Friedrich Wagemann, and Christoph
  94: Wockel, *Making Lifting Obstructions Explicit*, Proceedings of the London
  95: Mathematical Society 106(3) (2013), 589--620, DOI
  96: `10.1112/plms/pds047`, arXiv:`1108.5853`.
  97: 
  98: The crossed-module comparison discussed in connection with that work is an
  99: explicitly closed route for this paper. This paper begins only after a gerbe
 100: `H^2` class and a Cech representative of that class have been supplied. It
 101: does not contain the source-side degree-three locally continuous or locally
 102: smooth group-cohomology chain model, the characteristic 3-cocycle derived
 103: from strict crossed-module data, or the comparison cochain needed to identify
 104: its Cechization with the lifting-gerbe 2-cocycle.
 105: 
 106: In particular, the missing step is not supplied by the paper's UCT images,
 107: finite quotients, banded-equivalence naturality, or wedge calculation. It
 108: would require an explicit cochain comparison, with conventions, choice
 109: independence, refinement compatibility, and naturality proved at chain level.
 110: The current paper therefore neither derives a gerbe class from crossed-module
 111: data nor claims to solve that comparison problem. Pursuing the comparison
 112: would require a separate source-side theory and a separate proof, not an
 113: extension of the results recorded here.
 114:

 succeeded in 239ms:
   1: \section{The wedge-of-spheres realization corollary}
   2: \label{sec:wedge-application}
   3: 
   4: Fix \(\beta\ge1\), and let \(K_\beta\) be a finite connected simplicial
   5: complex with
   6: \[
   7:  |K_\beta|\simeq\bigvee_{j=1}^{\beta}S^2.
   8: \]
   9: For each \(m\ge0\), take the open-star cover of
  10: \(\operatorname{Sd}^mK_\beta\), and let \(\mathcal B_\beta\) be the
  11: all-subdivision basis site of
  12: \Cref{prop:finite-site-comparison}(iii).  Every nonempty finite overlap is
  13: contractible, hence connected, and the coherent coefficient trivializations
  14: are those of the genuine constant sheaf.  Thus the hypothesis omitted by an
  15: ordinary-nerve formulation with disconnected overlaps is satisfied here.
  16: 
  17: \subsection*{The selected geometric presentation}
  18: 
  19: The vertices of \(\operatorname{Sd}^mK_\beta\) index its open-star cover.
  20: A finite family of open stars has nonempty intersection exactly when the
  21: corresponding vertices span a simplex.  The nerve of the cover is therefore
  22: canonically isomorphic to \(\operatorname{Sd}^mK_\beta\).  In particular,
  23: \[
  24:  |N(\mathcal U^{(m)})|
  25:  \cong|\operatorname{Sd}^mK_\beta|
  26:  \cong|K_\beta|.
  27: \]
  28: The homology groups used below are consequently
  29: \begin{equation}\label{eq:wedge-homology}
  30:  H_1(N(\mathcal U^{(m)}),\mathbb Z)=0,
  31:  \qquad
  32:  H_2(N(\mathcal U^{(m)}),\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta.
  33: \end{equation}
  34: 
  35: If vertices \(v_0,\dots,v_q\) span a simplex, the intersection of their open
  36: stars is the open star of that simplex.  It deformation retracts to the
  37: barycentre and is connected and contractible.  If they do not span a
  38: simplex, their intersection is empty and is represented by the strict
  39: initial object of the finite intersection site.  Hence each selected cover
  40: satisfies the connected-overlap hypothesis literally, not only after passage
  41: to a component nerve.
  42: 
  43: The subdivision maps provide a filtered system of refinements.  The basis
  44: comparison does not assert that a homological image subgroup is independent
  45: of every possible refinement zigzag.  It says that each class on the
  46: underlying polyhedron has representatives on sufficiently fine selected
  47: covers and that the associated derived class is the image of those
  48: representatives.  The corollary below fixes one such cover and applies the
  49: realization theorem there.
  50: 
  51: For two component classes with homological images \(K_1,K_2\le G\), the
  52: kernel of the aggregate comparison is, by
  53: \eqref{eq:aggregate-exact-sequence},
  54: \[
  55:  \frac{K_1+K_2}{K_1\cap K_2}.
  56: \]
  57: We ask when the selected realization construction makes this kernel equal to
  58: the band itself by requiring
  59: \begin{equation}\label{eq:complementary-images}
  60:  K_1\cap K_2=0,
  61:  \qquad K_1+K_2=G,
  62:  \qquad K_1,K_2\ne0.
  63: \end{equation}
  64: 
  65: \begin{corollary}[Two-component realization on a wedge of \(2\)-spheres]
  66: \label{thm:wedge-sphere-two-component-label-loss}
  67: Let \(G\) be a finite abelian group and let \(d(G)\) be its minimal number
  68: of generators.  The following are equivalent:
  69: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  70: \item \(G\ne0\), \(d(G)\le2\beta\), and \(G\) is not cyclic of
  71:       prime-power order;
  72: \item on one of the selected finite open-star covers in
  73:       \(\mathcal B_\beta\), the prestack construction of
  74:       \Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction} has two non-neutral
  75:       component gerbes whose homological images satisfy
  76:       \eqref{eq:complementary-images}.
  77: \end{enumerate}
  78: When these conditions hold,
  79: \[
  80:  Q_{\mathrm{lab}}\cong G,
  81:  \qquad Q_{\mathrm{com}}=0,
  82:  \qquad
  83:  \ker(Q_{\mathrm{lab}}\to Q_{\mathrm{com}})\cong G,
  84: \]
  85: and the constructed component presheaf has matching without amalgamation.
  86: The assertion is relative to this selected finite-site realization; it is not
  87: a presentation-independent classification of gerbes or finite abelian groups.
  88: \end{corollary}
  89: 
  90: \begin{proof}
  91: For every selected cover, the nerve is a barycentric subdivision of
  92: \(K_\beta\).  The nerve and basis-site comparisons of
  93: \Cref{prop:finite-site-comparison} give
  94: \[
  95:  H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0,
  96:  \qquad
  97:  H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta,
  98: \]
  99: and identify the finite Cech representatives with the corresponding derived
 100: classes on \(\mathcal B_\beta\).
 101: 
 102: Assume (ii).  By \Cref{prop:uct-aggregate-package}(iv), each \(K_i\) is
 103: generated by at most \(\beta\) elements.  The complementary-image equations
 104: give
 105: \[
 106:  G=K_1\oplus K_2.
 107: \]
 108: Hence \(d(G)\le d(K_1)+d(K_2)\le2\beta\).  Since both summands are nonzero,
 109: \(G\) is decomposable.  The indecomposable finite abelian groups are exactly
 110: the cyclic groups of prime-power order \cite[Chap.~12]{DummitFoote2004}, so

 succeeded in 238ms:
  90: \emph{\(\mathscr A\)-banded prestack lift of \(F\)} is a prestack in
  91: groupoids \(\mathfrak P\) with a fixed split cleavage, together with
  92: compatible identifications
  93: \[
  94:  \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P)\cong F,
  95:  \qquad
  96:  \underline{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\mathfrak P}(x)\cong\mathscr A
  97: \]
  98: on every slice on which \(x\) is defined.  Let
  99: \[
 100:  \iota:\mathfrak P\longrightarrow\mathfrak L:=a(\mathfrak P)
 101: \]
 102: be stackification.  Standard compatibility of stackification with connected
 103: components gives
 104: \begin{equation}\label{eq:stackification-components-core}
 105:  \pi_0(\mathfrak L)\cong aF,
 106: \end{equation}
 107: and the map induced by \(\iota\) on components is the sheafification unit.
 108: We say that \(\iota\) is \emph{terminally essentially surjective} if
 109: \(\mathfrak P(a)\to\mathfrak L(a)\) is essentially surjective.
 110: 
 111: The lift is \emph{representative-rigid} if there are maps
 112: \[
 113:  s_X:F(X)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ob}(\mathfrak P(X))
 114: \]
 115: selecting a representative of every component and satisfying, for every
 116: \(f:Y\to X\),
 117: \begin{equation}\label{eq:strict-representative-pullback}
 118:  f^*s_X(t)=s_Y(f^*t)
 119: \end{equation}
 120: as a literal equality.  The equality is taken with respect to the fixed
 121: split cleavage.  Ordinary choices of cartesian lifts are generally only
 122: pseudofunctorial; the strictness in
 123: \eqref{eq:strict-representative-pullback} is therefore a substantive extra
 124: hypothesis, not a harmless choice of notation.  Split cleavages and their
 125: relation to pseudofunctorial pullback are standard for fibred categories
 126: \cite[Chap.~VI]{MacLaneMoerdijk1994}.
 127: 
 128: A componentwise representative choice alone would instead provide objects
 129: \(s_X(t)\) and, for \(f:Y\to X\), some isomorphism
 130: \[
 131:  f^*s_X(t)\xrightarrow{\sim}s_Y(f^*t).
 132: \]
 133: On a composable pair \(Z\xrightarrow{g}Y\xrightarrow{f}X\), the two induced
 134: isomorphisms from \((fg)^*s_X(t)\) to \(s_Z((fg)^*t)\) need not agree.
 135: Their ratio is an automorphism, hence a section of the band.  On triple
 136: overlaps these ratios can contribute a nonzero \(2\)-cocycle.  The equality
 137: \eqref{eq:strict-representative-pullback} eliminates the comparison arrows
 138: and their defect.  The rigidity theorem uses exactly this elimination and
 139: does not claim that an arbitrary fibred category admits it.
 140:

 142: \label{prop:finite-site-semantic-interface}
 143: With the notation above:
 144: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
 145: \item a strict matching family determines a section of \((aF)(a)\); if
 146:       \(F\) is separated on the slice over \(a\), every section of
 147:       \((aF)(a)\) is represented by a strict matching family on a cover of

 149:       \[
 150:       \operatorname{Match}(F,a)
 151:       \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 152:       (aF)(a)\ne\varnothing;
 153:       \]
 154: \item \(\mathfrak P(a)\ne\varnothing\) if and only if \(F(a)\ne\varnothing\).
 155:       If \(\iota\) is terminally essentially surjective, these are also
 156:       equivalent to \(\mathfrak L(a)\ne\varnothing\);
 157: \item under separatedness on the slice and terminal essential surjectivity,
 158:       \(\operatorname{MWA}(F,a)\) holds exactly when \((aF)(a)\) is
 159:       nonempty and \(\mathfrak L(a)\) is empty.
 160: \end{enumerate}
 161: \end{proposition}
 162: 
 163: \begin{proof}
 164: Applying \(u\) to a strict matching family gives a matching family in the
 165: sheaf \(aF\), which has a unique amalgamation in \((aF)(a)\).  Conversely,
 166: let \(\xi\in(aF)(a)\).  The plus--plus description of sheafification gives
 167: a cover \(\{U_i\to a\}\) and sections \(s_i\in F(U_i)\) representing
 168: \(\xi|_{U_i}\), with the following local equality on each overlap: there is
 169: a cover \(\{V_{ijk}\to U_{ij}\}_k\) such that
 170: \[
 171:  s_i|_{V_{ijk}}=s_j|_{V_{ijk}}.
 172: \]
 173: Every \(U_{ij}\to a\) is an object of the slice.  Separatedness there turns
 174: these locally witnessed equalities into
 175: \(s_i|_{U_{ij}}=s_j|_{U_{ij}}\).  The \(s_i\) therefore form a strict

 succeeded in 251ms:
   1: \section{Presentation-comparison bookkeeping}
   2: \label{sec:presentation-bookkeeping}
   3: 
   4: The article deliberately keeps the homological image attached to supplied
   5: finite-site data.  This section records the limited naturality that is useful
   6: when two such presentations have been related explicitly.  It does not assert
   7: path independence for arbitrary zigzags and does not turn the homological
   8: image into an invariant of an unspecified geometric object.
   9: 
  10: Fix a finite abelian group \(A\).  A \emph{finite presentation} is a pair
  11: 
  12: \[
  13:  (N,\omega),
  14:  \qquad N\text{ a finite simplicial complex},\quad
  15:  \omega\in H^2(N,A).
  16: \]
  17: 
  18: Its evaluation homomorphism and image are
  19: 
  20: \[
  21:  e_\omega:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\longrightarrow A,
  22:  \qquad K_\omega=\operatorname{Im}(e_\omega).
  23: \]
  24: 
  25: The notation suppresses the universal coefficient transformation.  It is
  26: important that the source group remains visible: an equality between image
  27: subgroups can be justified by naturality only after the relevant map on
  28: homology and the relevant map on coefficients have both been specified.
  29: 
  30: We use three elementary comparison moves.
  31: 
  32: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  33: \item A \emph{retri-vialization move} replaces a normalized cocycle
  34:       \(\alpha\) by \(\alpha+\delta b\) on the same nerve.  It fixes the
  35:       class \(\omega=[\alpha]\), hence fixes \(e_\omega\) and
  36:       \(K_\omega\).
  37: \item A \emph{simplicial move} is a simplicial map \(f:N'\to N\), carrying
  38:       \((N,\omega)\) to \((N',f^*\omega)\).
  39: \item A \emph{coefficient move} is an isomorphism
  40:       \(\theta:A\xrightarrow{\sim}A'\), carrying \((N,\omega)\) to
  41:       \((N,\theta_*\omega)\).
  42: \end{enumerate}
  43: 
  44: Let \(\mathscr P_A\) denote the free groupoid generated by chosen invertible
  45: instances of these moves.  Thus a morphism in \(\mathscr P_A\) remembers its
  46: word in the selected comparison data.  Relations are imposed only for
  47: identity moves, inverses, and literal compositions of the underlying maps.
  48: In particular, two different refinement zigzags with the same endpoints are
  49: not declared equal merely because they compare the same pair of nerves.
  50: 
  51: \begin{proposition}[Naturality along a specified presentation comparison]
  52: \label{prop:presentation-comparison-naturality}
  53: Let a morphism in the presentation groupoid be represented by a simplicial
  54: isomorphism \(f:N'\to N\), a coefficient isomorphism
  55: \(\theta:A\to A'\), and any number of retrivialization moves, so that
  56: 
  57: \[
  58:  \omega'=\theta_*f^*\omega\in H^2(N',A').
  59: \]
  60: 
  61: Then
  62: 
  63: \begin{equation}\label{eq:presentation-evaluation-square}
  64: \begin{CD}
  65: H_2(N',\mathbb Z) @>{e_{\omega'}}>> A'\\
  66: @V{f_*}V{\cong}V @A{\theta}A{\cong}A\\
  67: H_2(N,\mathbb Z) @>{e_\omega}>> A
  68: \end{CD}
  69: \end{equation}
  70: 
  71: commutes.  Consequently
  72: 
  73: \[
  74:  K_{\omega'}=\theta(K_\omega),
  75:  \qquad
  76:  A'/K_{\omega'}\cong A/K_\omega.
  77: \]
  78: 
  79: These identifications are functorial in the chosen word of comparison moves.
  80: No equality between the identifications attached to distinct words is
  81: asserted without an additional coherence argument.
  82: \end{proposition}
  83: 
  84: \begin{proof}
  85: The universal coefficient map is natural in the simplicial complex and in
  86: the coefficient group.  If \(z\in H_2(N',\mathbb Z)\), then
  87: 
  88: \[
  89:  e_{\omega'}(z)
  90:  =e_{\theta_*f^*\omega}(z)
  91:  =\theta\bigl(e_\omega(f_*z)\bigr),
  92: \]
  93: 
  94: which is the commutativity of
  95: \eqref{eq:presentation-evaluation-square}.  Since \(f_*\) and \(\theta\)
  96: are isomorphisms, taking images gives
  97: \(K_{\omega'}=\theta(K_\omega)\).  The induced quotient isomorphism is
  98: \(a+K_\omega\mapsto\theta(a)+K_{\omega'}\).
  99: 
 100: A retrivialization changes a cocycle by a coboundary and hence does not
 101: change its cohomology class.  The calculation therefore applies to each
 102: generator.  Naturality under a composite follows by pasting the corresponding
 103: squares.  Because the groupoid is free on the selected moves, this proves
 104: functoriality along the recorded word and nothing stronger.
 105: \end{proof}
 106: 
 107: The same calculation applies componentwise to the family in
 108: \Cref{art-thm:prescribed-component-image-construction}.  Sums and
 109: intersections are carried through a coefficient isomorphism, so the two
 110: aggregate quotients in \Cref{art-prop:uct-aggregate-package} are transported
 111: as well.  This is the entire presentation-comparison claim used by the
 112: article.

 116: For completeness, choose a total order on the vertex set of \(N\).  A
 117: normalized \(2\)-cochain is a function
 118: 
 119: \[
 120:  \alpha_{ijk}\in A

  81: def _divisors(n):
  82:     return tuple(d for d in range(1, n + 1) if n % d == 0)

  85: def _cyclic_subgroup(n, generators):
  86:     divisor = reduce(gcd, generators, n)

  91:     factorization_cases = 0
  92:     exact_sequence_cases = 0
  93:

  95:         subgroups = tuple(_cyclic_subgroup(n, (d,)) for d in _divisors(n))

 100:                     u for u in range(quotient_modulus) if gcd(u, quotient_modulus) == 1
 101:                 ) or (0,)

 105:                         x
 106:                         for x in range(n)
 107:                         if (unit * x) % quotient_modulus == 0

 109:                     pushed_evaluation_is_zero = kills_image
 110:                     assert kills_image == factors_through == pushed_evaluation_is_zero
 111:                     factorization_cases += 1
 112: 
 113:         for first, second, third in product(subgroups, repeat=3):
 114:             intersection = first & second & third
 115:             subgroup_sum = _cyclic_subgroup(n, tuple(first | second | third))
 116:             assert intersection <= subgroup_sum
 117: 
 118:             labelled_quotient_order = n // len(intersection)
 119:             common_quotient_order = n // len(subgroup_sum)
 120:             kernel_order = len(subgroup_sum) // len(intersection)
 121:             assert labelled_quotient_order == kernel_order * common_quotient_order
 122:             exact_sequence_cases += 1
 123: 
 124:     return {
 125:         "factorization_cases": factorization_cases,
 126:         "exact_sequence_cases": exact_sequence_cases,
 127:     }

 130: def _elements(moduli):
 131:     return tuple(product(*(range(modulus) for modulus in moduli)))
 132: 
 133: 
 134: def _add(left, right, moduli):
 135:     return tuple((x + y) % modulus for x, y, modulus in zip(left, right, moduli))
 136: 
 137: 
 138: def _generated_subgroup(moduli, generators):
 139:     zero = tuple(0 for _ in moduli)
 140:     generated = {zero}
 141:     frontier = [zero]
 142:     while frontier:
 143:         current = frontier.pop()
 144:         for generator in generators:
 145:             candidate = _add(current, generator, moduli)
 146:             if candidate not in generated:
 147:                 generated.add(candidate)
 148:                 frontier.append(candidate)
 149:     return frozenset(generated)
 150:

 152: def _all_subgroups(moduli):
 153:     elements = _elements(moduli)
 154:     zero = tuple(0 for _ in moduli)
 155:     subgroups = []
 156:     for size in range(1, len(elements) + 1):
 157:         for subset_tuple in combinations(elements, size):
 158:             subset = frozenset(subset_tuple)
 159:             if zero not in subset:
 160:                 continue
 161:             if all(_add(x, y, moduli) in subset for x in subset for y in subset):
 162:                 subgroups.append(subset)
 163:     return tuple(subgroups)
 164:

 167:     elements = tuple(subgroup)
 168:     for beta in range(len(elements) + 1):
 169:         if any(
 170:             _generated_subgroup(moduli, generators) == subgroup
 171:             for generators in product(elements, repeat=beta)
 172:         ):
 173:             return beta
 174:     raise AssertionError("finite subgroup has no finite generating tuple")

 219: def action_lift_peiffer_counterexample():
 220:     # G = K = C_2 and K-hat = C_2 x C_2 with boundary (a,b) |-> a.
 221:     def boundary(element):
 222:         return element[0]
 223: 
 224:     def action(group_element, element):
 225:         a, b = element
 226:         return (a, (b + group_element * a) % 2)
 227: 
 228:     def multiply(left, right):
 229:         return ((left[0] + right[0]) % 2, (left[1] + right[1]) % 2)
 230: 
 231:     def inverse(element):
 232:         return element  # Every element of C_2 x C_2 has order at most two.
 233: 
 234:     elements = tuple(product(range(2), repeat=2))
 235:     covers_conjugation = all(
 236:         boundary(action(g, element)) == boundary(element)
 237:         for g in range(2)
 238:         for element in elements
 239:     )
 240:     fixes_central_kernel = all(
 241:         action(g, (0, z)) == (0, z) for g in range(2) for z in range(2)
 242:     )
 243:     peiffer_identity = all(
 244:         action(boundary(left), right)
 245:         == multiply(multiply(left, right), inverse(left))
 246:         for left in elements
 247:         for right in elements
 248:     )
 249:     assert covers_conjugation and fixes_central_kernel and not peiffer_identity
 250:     return {
 251:         "covers_conjugation": covers_conjugation,
 252:         "fixes_central_kernel": fixes_central_kernel,
 253:         "peiffer_identity": peiffer_identity,
 254:     }

codex
The full target does not close as stated. There is a structural contradiction in the neutral terminal fibre, before refinement coherence becomes an issue: if \(\pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P)(X)=\Lambda_0\) and \(\mathfrak P(X)\to a\mathfrak P(X)\) is essentially surjective, then each neutral component of \(a\mathfrak P(X)\) has only one isomorphism class; but a neutral \(\mathcal A\)-gerbe has global isomorphism classes forming a torsor under \(H^1(X,\mathcal A)\). Thus the stated clauses force \(H^1(X,\mathcal A)=0\), contrary to the theorem’s allowed data and its claimed sharp \(H^1\) ambiguity. I’m formalizing this obstruction at referee standard and will run the complete clean-build and artifact verification regimen without relabelling a weakened construction as intrinsic.
codex
I am adding a standalone obstruction report under `artifacts/` rather than changing the paper’s theorem statements. It proves the incompatibility, gives the concrete counterexample \(X=S^1\), \(\mathcal A=\underline{\mathbb Z/2}\), \(\omega=0\), and records that the cover-level twisted composition and refinement formulas are viable while the required terminal essential-surjectivity clause is not.

+# Obstruction to Theorem A as stated
+
+## Outcome
+
+Theorem A does not close in the form stated in `oracle_sprint_A9_tier.md`.
+The obstruction is in the simultaneous requirements on the neutral terminal
+fibre and the claimed `H^1` ambiguity, not in the disconnected-overlap
+cocycle formula or in refinement coherence.
+
+## No-go proposition
+
+Let `X` be a site with terminal object, let `A` be an abelian sheaf, let `P`
+be an `A`-banded prestack, and let `L = aP` be its stackification. Fix a
+global component `lambda` such that the component gerbe `L[lambda]` is
+neutral. Assume:
+
+1. the fibrewise isomorphism classes of objects of `P(X)` in the component
+   `lambda` form a singleton; and
+2. `P(X) -> L(X)` is essentially surjective on that component.
+
+Then `H^1(X,A) = 0`.
+
+### Proof
+
+By the first assumption, any two objects of `P(X)` in the component
+`lambda` are isomorphic in `P(X)`. Their images are therefore isomorphic in
+`L(X)`. By essential surjectivity, every object of `L[lambda](X)` is
+isomorphic to the image of an object of `P(X)`. Hence any two objects of
+`L[lambda](X)` are isomorphic, so
+
+```text
+pi_0(L[lambda](X))
+```
+
+is a singleton.
+
+Choose a global object `x` of the neutral `A`-gerbe `L[lambda]`. Twisting
+`x` by an `A`-torsor, and sending an object `y` to the torsor
+`Isom(x,y)`, are mutually inverse on isomorphism classes. Consequently
+
+```text
+pi_0(L[lambda](X))  is a torsor under  H^1(X,A).
+```
+
+Because this torsor is a singleton, `H^1(X,A)` is the trivial group. This
+proves the proposition.
+
+The twisting statement used here is the standard neutral-gerbe equivalence:
+after a global object is fixed, an abelian `A`-banded neutral gerbe is
+equivalent to the stack of `A`-torsors. It is the same standard fact that
+identifies the isomorphism classes of global objects with `H^1(X,A)` and
+the automorphism group of a fixed global object with `H^0(X,A)`.
+
+## Contradiction with the target
+
+Theorem A requires
+
+```text
+pi_0^pre(P)(X) = Lambda_0
+```
+
+and terminal essential surjectivity. For each `lambda` in `Lambda_0`, the
+first equality says that the terminal prestack fibre has exactly one
+isomorphism class in that marked component. The no-go proposition therefore
+forces `H^1(X,A) = 0` whenever `Lambda_0` is nonempty.
+
+This is false for the allowed standard data. Take
+
+```text
+X = S^1,
+A = the constant local system Z/2,
+Lambda = {lambda},
+omega_lambda = 0.
+```
+
+Then `Lambda_0 = Lambda` and
+
+```text
+H^1(S^1,Z/2) = Z/2.
+```
+
+Finite Leray covers exist and the zero class has Cech representatives and
+null-homotopies on them, so this example satisfies the stated hypotheses.
+The neutral component gerbe has two isomorphism classes of global objects.
+A terminal prestack fibre with one isomorphism class cannot map essentially
+surjectively to both.
+
+Thus the target's `H^1` ambiguity is not merely an ambiguity between
+presentation comparisons. It already appears as the unavoidable set of
+global-object isomorphism classes in every neutral component. The target
+simultaneously requires that ambiguity to exist and requires the terminal
+prestack fibre to erase it.
+
+## What survives
+
+The representative-rigidity theorem carries over unchanged. Its hypotheses
+and proof are formulated on an arbitrary site with an abelian sheaf band;
+they do not use connected intersections or an ordinary nerve.
+
+The local twisted-composition calculation also survives after one necessary
+rebuild. On a possibly disconnected intersection `W`, arrow coordinates lie
+in the actual group `Gamma(W,A)`. For chart indices `i,j,k`, composition is
+
+```text
+(d,j,k) o (c,i,j) = (c + d + alpha_ijk|W, i, k).
+```
+
+Associativity is exactly `delta alpha = 0`, now as an equality of sections
+on every component of the overlap. Restriction applies the sheaf restriction
+map to both arrow coordinates and cocycle sections. A gauge
+`alpha' = alpha + delta c` subtracts `c_ij` from arrow coordinates, and a
+change `c' = c + delta e` gives the natural transformation with component
+`-e_i`. These formulas respect identities, addition, and composition.
+
+For a refinement map `r: V -> U`, sending a chart index `j` to `r(j)` and
+restricting coefficient sections gives the comparison functor. For
+composable refinements these formulas agree literally with the formula for
+the composite refinement, so unit and associativity coherence can be made
+strict. Actual section groups, rather than one constant group per ordinary
+nerve simplex, are essential here.
+
+After localization over finite Leray covers, two cochain gauges between the
+same unmarked gerbe presentations differ by a Cech `1`-cocycle. Their
+equivalence classes form a torsor under `H^1(X,A)`, and the automorphisms of
+a fixed gauge are the Cech `0`-cocycles, namely `H^0(X,A)`. This gives the
+claimed sharp ambiguity for stackification comparisons. It does not repair
+the incompatible terminal-fibre clauses proved above.
+
+## Minimal mathematical repairs, none adopted here
+
+Any one of the following would remove the contradiction, but each weakens or
+changes the requested theorem:
+
+1. assume `H^1(X,A) = 0` whenever `Lambda_0` is nonempty;
+2. drop terminal essential surjectivity;
+3. replace `pi_0^pre(P)(X) = Lambda_0` by a terminal fibre containing the
+   full `H^1(X,A)`-torsor of isomorphism classes in every neutral component;
+4. weaken essential surjectivity to surjectivity only on marked component
+   labels.
+
+The requested rules expressly disallow silently making such a compromise.
+Accordingly, no presentation-relative or weakened construction has been
+inserted into the manuscript under the name of Theorem A.

+# Obstruction to Theorem A as stated
+
+## Outcome
+
+Theorem A does not close in the form stated in `oracle_sprint_A9_tier.md`.
+The obstruction is in the simultaneous requirements on the neutral terminal
+fibre and the claimed `H^1` ambiguity, not in the disconnected-overlap
+cocycle formula or in refinement coherence.
+
+## No-go proposition
+
+Let `X` be a site with terminal object, let `A` be an abelian sheaf, let `P`
+be an `A`-banded prestack, and let `L = aP` be its stackification. Fix a
+global component `lambda` such that the component gerbe `L[lambda]` is
+neutral. Assume:
+
+1. the fibrewise isomorphism classes of objects of `P(X)` in the component
+   `lambda` form a singleton; and
+2. `P(X) -> L(X)` is essentially surjective on that component.
+
+Then `H^1(X,A) = 0`.
+
+### Proof
+
+By the first assumption, any two objects of `P(X)` in the component
+`lambda` are isomorphic in `P(X)`. Their images are therefore isomorphic in
+`L(X)`. By essential surjectivity, every object of `L[lambda](X)` is
+isomorphic to the image of an object of `P(X)`. Hence any two objects of
+`L[lambda](X)` are isomorphic, so
+
+```text
+pi_0(L[lambda](X))
+```
+
+is a singleton.
+
+Choose a global object `x` of the neutral `A`-gerbe `L[lambda]`. Twisting
+`x` by an `A`-torsor, and sending an object `y` to the torsor
+`Isom(x,y)`, are mutually inverse on isomorphism classes. Consequently
+
+```text
+pi_0(L[lambda](X))  is a torsor under  H^1(X,A).
+```
+
+Because this torsor is a singleton, `H^1(X,A)` is the trivial group. This
+proves the proposition.
+
+The twisting statement used here is the standard neutral-gerbe equivalence:
+after a global object is fixed, an abelian `A`-banded neutral gerbe is
+equivalent to the stack of `A`-torsors. It is the same standard fact that
+identifies the isomorphism classes of global objects with `H^1(X,A)` and
+the automorphism group of a fixed global object with `H^0(X,A)`.
+
+## Contradiction with the target
+
+Theorem A requires
+
+```text
+pi_0^pre(P)(X) = Lambda_0
+```
+
+and terminal essential surjectivity. For each `lambda` in `Lambda_0`, the
+first equality says that the terminal prestack fibre has exactly one
+isomorphism class in that marked component. The no-go proposition therefore
+forces `H^1(X,A) = 0` whenever `Lambda_0` is nonempty.
+
+This is false for the allowed standard data. Take
+
+```text
+X = S^1,
+A = the constant local system Z/2,
+Lambda = {lambda},
+omega_lambda = 0.
+```
+
+Then `Lambda_0 = Lambda` and
+
+```text
+H^1(S^1,Z/2) = Z/2.
+```
+
+Finite Leray covers exist and the zero class has Cech representatives and
+null-homotopies on them, so this example satisfies the stated hypotheses.
+The neutral component gerbe has two isomorphism classes of global objects.
+A terminal prestack fibre with one isomorphism class cannot map essentially
+surjectively to both.
+
+Thus the target's `H^1` ambiguity is not merely an ambiguity between
+presentation comparisons. It already appears as the unavoidable set of
+global-object isomorphism classes in every neutral component. The target
+simultaneously requires that ambiguity to exist and requires the terminal
+prestack fibre to erase it.
+
+## What survives
+
+The representative-rigidity theorem carries over unchanged. Its hypotheses
+and proof are formulated on an arbitrary site with an abelian sheaf band;
+they do not use connected intersections or an ordinary nerve.
+
+The local twisted-composition calculation also survives after one necessary
+rebuild. On a possibly disconnected intersection `W`, arrow coordinates lie
+in the actual group `Gamma(W,A)`. For chart indices `i,j,k`, composition is
+
+```text
+(d,j,k) o (c,i,j) = (c + d + alpha_ijk|W, i, k).
+```
+
+Associativity is exactly `delta alpha = 0`, now as an equality of sections
+on every component of the overlap. Restriction applies the sheaf restriction
+map to both arrow coordinates and cocycle sections. A gauge
+`alpha' = alpha + delta c` subtracts `c_ij` from arrow coordinates, and a
+change `c' = c + delta e` gives the natural transformation with component
+`-e_i`. These formulas respect identities, addition, and composition.
+
+For a refinement map `r: V -> U`, sending a chart index `j` to `r(j)` and
+restricting coefficient sections gives the comparison functor. For
+composable refinements these formulas agree literally with the formula for
+the composite refinement, so unit and associativity coherence can be made
+strict. Actual section groups, rather than one constant group per ordinary
+nerve simplex, are essential here.
+
+After localization over finite Leray covers, two cochain gauges between the
+same unmarked gerbe presentations differ by a Cech `1`-cocycle. Their
+equivalence classes form a torsor under `H^1(X,A)`, and the automorphisms of
+a fixed gauge are the Cech `0`-cocycles, namely `H^0(X,A)`. This gives the
+claimed sharp ambiguity for stackification comparisons. It does not repair
+the incompatible terminal-fibre clauses proved above.
+
+## Minimal mathematical repairs, none adopted here
+
+Any one of the following would remove the contradiction, but each weakens or
+changes the requested theorem:
+
+1. assume `H^1(X,A) = 0` whenever `Lambda_0` is nonempty;
+2. drop terminal essential surjectivity;
+3. replace `pi_0^pre(P)(X) = Lambda_0` by a terminal fibre containing the
+   full `H^1(X,A)`-torsor of isomorphism classes in every neutral component;
+4. weaken essential surjectivity to surjectivity only on marked component
+   labels.
+
+The requested rules expressly disallow silently making such a compromise.
+Accordingly, no presentation-relative or weakened construction has been
+inserted into the manuscript under the name of Theorem A.
